The spectral site revisited
Axel Osmond

Abstract
We give the site-theoretic account of the spectral construction as first introduced by Coste. We provide a detailed examination of the geometric properties of the spectrum, in particular what classes of topoi it produces when applied to the different classes of objects and maps in a geometry. We also give a new proof of the spectral adjunction for set-valued models and the classifying property of the structure sheaf. We also discuss the opposition between “gros and petit” spectra and relation with some canonical classifying sites. We then describe the general case of a modelled topos. We also prove that the spectrum of a locally modelled topos is local over its base and deduce a new proof of the spectral adjunction for the general case. We finally give several examples from the world of propositional algebras, and in particular recover classifying topoi of first order theories in various fragment of logic as spectra of some suitably modelled topoi. Keywords. Spectrum, geometry, (locally) modelled topos, spectral site, fibered site. MSC2020. 14A20, 06D50, 18F10, 03G30.



Introduction

This work, which derives directly from chapter 7 and 9 of the author’s thesis
[
21
]
, provides an in-depth description of the geometric properties of the spectrum associated with a geometry. Several manner exists to construct spectra, either an abstract way through 2-dimensional universal properties as in
[
5
]
or
[
11
]
, focusing on its classifying purpose though without actually any insight on the spatial aspects of the spectrum, or more a more concrete pointset manner as in
[
9
]
; this paper deals somewhat with a middle-way, the site-theoretic approach of the construction of the spectrum. Such an approach combine both the advantages of giving a spatial insight and encoding the universal properties one expect from the spectrum. Though we start from the recipe given in
[
6
]
(and the akin approach in
[
1
]
) when defining the spectral site, most of our the work is done in a new manner, developing many aspects that were left mostly implicit our sources.

A first section gives all required notions about geometries and a few generalities about factorization systems and geometric extensions as we are going to use throughout this paper. Most of this is treated in a more detailed way in a upcoming companion paper, also in the first three chapters of
[
21
]
.

In section 2 we give the spectral site of set-valued models, which gathers the finitely presented etale maps under it, and prove it possesses the desired universal property of the spectrum: in particular it induces a restricted spectral adjunction
theorem

2.7.2
. Then we provide a pseudolimit decomposition
theorem

2.2.7
for the spectrum of arbitrary etale maps, and give some geometric properties of the spectra, proving in particular that the spectrum turns etale maps into etale geometric morphisms (
proposition

2.2.2
) and local objects into local topoi (
proposition

2.3.7
). We also give a careful examination of the universal property of the structure sheaf and its associated
generic etale map
and
generic local unit
, from which we deduce a restricted spectral adjunction for set-valued models. We also give presentation sites for topoi classifying either etale maps and (etale) maps toward a local object without fixing the domain, and discuss also the relation between gros and petit topos.

In section 3, we provide a detailed account of the spectral site of an arbitrary modelled topos. We also use fibrational techniques to prove the structure sheaf of the spectrum to be a local object. We also describe the geometric and sheaf data associated to the canonical fibration, which will be proven to be the unit of the spectral adjunction.

Section 4 is devoted to proving the pseudofunctoriality of the spectrum: though this aspect was somewhat swept under the rug in most sources, this problem is actually highly non trivial and requires a large amount of work, involving some techniques of extensions of models along the equivalence between a sheaf topos and its own category of sheaves for its canonical topology, and splitting the problem between the
horizontal and vertical
morphisms of modelled topoi as they were distinguished at
1.4.3
.

Section 5 is devoted to proving the spectral adjunction in the general case, for arbitrary modelled topoi, combining the two previous sections. The main lemma is to prove the existence of a retraction of the unit for locally modelled topoi (see
proposition

6.1.2
), which generalizes also the localness of the spectrum of a locally modelled topos, but this time
over its base topos
– see
corollary

6.1.4
.

Section 6 describes the functoriality of this method relative to transformations of geometries, and how we get a
comparison
functor between the spectra associated with two different geometries related by a transformation.

Finally, section 7 gather examples from Stone-like duality, some of them were not until now very well known as instance of the spectral construction. We also exploit those examples to construct classifying topoi of theory in various fragment of first order logic as spectra of modelled topoi consisting of the classifyier for the theory of objects (or its multi-sorted analogs) together with some internal Lindenbaum-Tarksi algebra eligible to a spectral construction or another. Finally we present the subobject hyperdoctrines associated to syntactic categories of first order theories as instances of modelled topoi and describe how their spectra are related to the classifying topoi of those theories through local geometric morphisms.

Ackonowledgements

The author is very grateful to Dorette Pronk and Eduardo Dubuc for their reviewing efforts of the thesis from which most of this paper originates. He would like also to thanks Mathieu Anel for numerous discussions and observations that were crucial in our comprehension of this theory, and he ackowledges that this work is very indebted to
[
1
]
. He should also thanks Olivia Caramello for discussions about various aspect of topos theory involved in this work. Finally he also thanks Morgan Rogers for helpful comments on several points of this topic.

1
Geometries and (locally) modelled topoi

In this section we recapitulate the essential about geometries (aka. admissibility structures), from which we are going to build spectral functors in the main part of the paper. It is primarily a concentrate of chapters 1, 3 and 6 of
[
21
]
treating respectively of left generated factorization systems, geometries proper and categories of modelled topoi.

We first expose, as a preliminary definition, the following version of the notion of
geometry
, which encapsulates the categorical and logical data from which will be extracted the geometric content of each spectral construction. Though several possible formulations exist, emphasizing either the logical, categorical or geometric aspect, we chose here to follow
[
6
]
definition (though called here
triple
or also
admissibility structure
) though using
[
18
]
terminology for its evocative virtue.

Definition 1.0.1
.

A
geometry
is the data of

-

a finite limit theory
\mathbb{T}

-

a
saturated class

\mathcal{V}
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]

-

a Grothendieck pretopology
J
on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}
whose covers are made duals of maps in
\mathcal{V}
.

This definitions involves three interdependent data, which will be unfolded :

-

the finite limit theory will code for a class of
ambient objects
; in particular the category of its set-valued model will be a locally finitely presentable category, but we are going to consider more generally the category of all possible models regardless of their base topos;

-

the saturated class (whose definition will be given in the next subsection) will be a class of finitely presented maps from which one will generate a factorization system in categories of models of the theory of ambient objects in any Grothendieck topoi;

-

the Grothendieck pretopology can also be visualized as a geometric extension of the theory of ambient objects; its models will be called
local objects
. The crucial condition of being generated in the saturated class will enable a special interaction between local objects and factorization.

Let us now recall briefly the content of the notions involved in this definition. First, the factorization data encoded in the saturated class:

1.1
Left generated factorization systems

Definition 1.1.1
.

A
saturated class
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
is a set
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]^{2}_{\omega}
of finitely presented maps such that:

-

\mathcal{V}
contains isomorphisms and is stable under composition,

-

\mathcal{V}
is right-cancellative

-

\mathcal{V}
is closed under finite colimits in
{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}^{2}

-

\mathcal{V}
is closed under pushouts along arbitrary maps between finitely presented objects

Remark 1.1.2
.

From its definition, a saturated class is always small as consisting of finitely presented maps.

In several sources were described process to induce from a saturated class a factorization system in a locally presentable category, as in
[
6
]
,
[
1
]
(also summed up in
[
21
]
[section 1.3]). This process involves the class of all left maps that are of finite presentation in the coslice under each object, which will generate arbitrary left maps out of this objects.

Definition 1.1.3
.

For any object
B
in
\mathcal{B}
, define the
etale generator at
B
as full subcategory
\mathcal{V}_{B}
of
B\downarrow\mathcal{B}
consisting of morphisms
n:B\rightarrow C
such that there exists some
l:K\rightarrow K^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{V}
and
a:K\rightarrow B
exhibiting
n
as the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

1.1.4
.

The etale generator
\mathcal{V}_{B}
can be shown to consists of finitely presented objects of in
B\downarrow\mathcal{B}
and to be closed under finite colimits. Hence we know the category
{\rm Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{B})
to be locally finitely presentable: its objects consists of maps
l:B\rightarrow C
under
B
that are filtered colimits of maps in
\mathcal{V}_{B}
.

Proposition 1.1.5

(
[
21
]
[Proposition 1.1.3.2)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a finite limit theory and
\mathcal{V}
be a saturated class in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
: then any arrow
f:B\rightarrow C
in
\mathcal{B}
admits a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\mathcal{V}_{B}\downarrow f}{{\textup{colim}}}\,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{colim}}\,\mathcal{V}_{B}\downarrow f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{f}}

with
l_{f}
is in
{\rm Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{B})
and
r_{f}
is in
\mathcal{V}^{\perp}
.

The interest of left generated factorization systems is that, being axiomatizable by a finite limit theory encoded by the small lex category
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}
, they are inherited in the categories of models in Grothendieck topoi beyond
\mathcal{S}
; moreover not only the left maps, but also the right maps behave as nicely as possible: in particular we will see that both left and right maps are stable under inverse and direct images of geometric morphisms, and that the factorizations in sheaf topoi are computed essentially component-wise. Complete proofs of this section can be found at
[
21
]
[Section 3.3.2].

1.1.6
.

For a left generated factorization system
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
generated from a saturated class
\mathcal{V}
, the associated factorization system, the class of finitely presented left maps
\mathcal{V}
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}
is dual to a class of morphisms in the syntactic category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}
. That is, an arrow
n:K_{\phi}\rightarrow K_{\psi}
(with
\phi
,
\psi
the presentation formulas of the domain and codomains) in
\mathcal{V}
corresponds to an arrow
[\theta_{n}(\overline{x},\overline{y})]_{\mathbb{T}}:\{\overline{y},\psi\}\rightarrow\{\overline{x},\phi\}
, which, as a
\mathbb{T}
-provably functional formula, should be seen as a function symbol coding for an operation.

1.1.7
.

The previous items were stated in the context of a locally finitely presentable category
\mathcal{B}
: we now abstract ourselves from this and work at the level of the underlying finite limit theory
\mathbb{T}
. From the definition of a saturated class, the category
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}
has finite limits, hence codes itself for a finite limit theory which admits as classifying topos
\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}}=[\mathcal{V},\mathcal{S}]
which we denote as
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{L}]
. In particular this allows us to define for each Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
a class of arrows
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{E}]
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
as

\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{E}]\simeq{\bf Geom}\big[\mathcal{E},\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{L}]\big]\simeq{\bf Lex}[\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}},\mathcal{E}]

As well as it is sufficient to test the orthogonality property of right maps between set-valued models against only the finitely presented etale maps, right maps in category of models in arbitrary topoi also can be characterized in a way only involving the saturated class rather than all left maps:

Proposition 1.1.8
.

For a Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
, a morphism in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
, seen as a natural transformation in
{\bf Lex}[\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}},\mathcal{E}]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

is right orthogonal to
\mathcal{V}
if and only if its naturality square at a morphism
[\theta_{n}(\overline{x},\overline{y})]_{\mathbb{T}}:\{\overline{y},\psi\}\rightarrow\{\overline{x},\phi\}
dual of a morphism
n
in
\mathcal{V}
is a pullback in
\mathcal{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\{\overline{y},\psi\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(\{\overline{y},\psi\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(\{\overline{x},\phi\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(\{\overline{x},\phi\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F([\theta_{n}(\overline{x},\overline{y})]_{\mathbb{T}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{\{\overline{y},\psi\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E([\theta_{n}(\overline{x},\overline{y})]_{\mathbb{T}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{\{\overline{x},\phi\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

In the following we denote as
\mathcal{R}[\mathcal{E}]
the class of right maps in
\mathcal{E}
.

Proposition 1.1.9

(
[
21
]
[Proposition 3.3.2.9])
.

For a left generated factorization system, right maps are stable under inverse image: any geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
induces a functor
f^{*}:\mathcal{R}[\mathcal{E}]\rightarrow\mathcal{R}[\mathcal{F}]
.

Now, recall that models of finite limit theories in sheaf topoi are sheaves of set-valued models over the base site, and morphisms between them are natural transformation. In particular we have:

Proposition 1.1.10

(
[
21
]
[Proposition 3.3.2.10] )
.

If
\mathcal{E}\simeq{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}},J_{\mathcal{E}})
, then a transformation
u:F\rightarrow E
in
\mathcal{E}
is a right map if and only if for any object
c
in
\mathcal{C}
, the component
u_{c}:F(c)\rightarrow E(c)
is in
\mathcal{R}
.

Now it appears that the factorization structure
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})
generated from
\mathcal{V}
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
is inherited in the category
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
in any Grothendieck topos, and moreover in a functorial and point-wise way. The point-wiseness of this factorization was first established at
[
6
]
[Theorem 3.6.3].

Proposition 1.1.11
.

For any Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
with a standard site of presentation
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}},J_{\mathcal{E}})
, we have a factorization system
(\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{E}],\mathcal{R}[\mathcal{E}])
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
. Moreover, for any
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
we have adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{E}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{F}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}[\mathcal{E}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}[\mathcal{F}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

1.1.12
.

In the remaining part of the paper, following a mixed terminology arising from different sources, we will give special name to the left and right classes induced from the saturated class
\mathcal{V}
in a geometry
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)
:

-

the left maps between set-valued
\mathbb{T}
-models will be called
etale maps
and we will denote their class as
{\bf Et}
. In particular the saturated class
\mathcal{V}
will coincide the class of finitely presented etale maps; more generally, for an arbitrary Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
, the class of etale maps in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
will be denoted
{\bf Et}[\mathcal{E}]
;

-

the right maps between set-valued
\mathbb{T}
-models will be called
local maps
and we will denote their class as
{\bf Loc}
; more generally, for an arbitrary Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
, the class of local maps in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
will be denoted
{\bf Loc}[\mathcal{E}]
.

1.2
Admissibility

Now we turn to the second component of the notion of geometry, the choice of a Grothendieck topology coding for some geometric extension.

1.2.1
.

Recall that a model of
\mathbb{T}_{J}
is an object in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
which is local relative to the dual in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}
of covering families in
J
. But the families dual to cover in
J
can be extended to the whole category
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
as follows:

Definition 1.2.2
.

Define the
generalized
J
-covers
as consisting, for each
B
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, of the families
({n_{i}}:B{\rightarrow}B_{i})_{i\in I}
such that there exists some
a:K\rightarrow B
and some family
(l_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
dual to a
J
-cover such that for each
i\in I
one has a pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then the property of injectiveness of local objects relatively to
J
-covers extends automatically to those extended covers (which was observed also in
[
1
]
[Lemma 22]):

1.2.3
.

An object
A
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
is

-local if and only if for any object

in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, any arrow
f:B\rightarrow A
and any generalized

(n_{i}:B\rightarrow B_{i})_{i\in I}
, there is a factorization of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists}

In fact
A
is
J
-local if and only if for any generalized cover under it
(n_{i}:A\rightarrow B_{i})_{i\in I}
, one has a retraction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}

Definition 1.2.4
.

For an object
B
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, a
local unit
of
B
is an etale map
n:B\rightarrow A
toward a
J
-local object.

Remark 1.2.5
.

Beware that local units are not required to be finitely presented in general. Etale maps will play the role of saturated compacts of the spectral topology, while finitely presented etale maps will play the role of basic compact opens from which we are going to construct the spectral topology. While this is not apparent in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
which is “on the algebraic side”, this is more intuitive in the opposite category
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]^{{\rm op}}
, whose objects should now be thought of as spaces, where the etale morphisms could be seen as generalized inclusions, and the generalized covers induced from
J
as covers over objects.

Remark 1.2.6
.

Local objects are like
focal spaces
, that is, spaces with a least point in the specialization order. For instance, in a topological space
X
and a point
x\in X
, the focal component of
X
in
x
is the intersection of all neighborhoods of
x
, and this is the upset
\uparrow x
in the specialization order. local units behave like inclusions of the form
\uparrow_{\sqsubseteq}x\hookrightarrow X
as such upsets are unreachable by open covering: indeed, in a cover of
\uparrow_{\sqsubseteq}x
, one open must contain
x
itself. But as open are up-sets for the specialization order, this open is the whole
\uparrow_{\sqsubseteq}x
. Hence maximal points, as they do not admit non trivial local units, are alike those
x
such that
\uparrow_{\sqsubseteq}x=\{x\}
.

In particular, triangles between local units

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}

should be seen as coding for specialization order between the corresponding minimal point
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
. Then in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]^{{\rm op}}
this will be turned into an inclusion of focal component
\uparrow x_{2}\subseteq\uparrow x_{1}
.

The generation condition in the axioms of geometry ensures the following key property relating local objects and local maps:

Lemma 1.2.7

(
[
21
]
[Lemma 3.3.1.8])
.

Any object
A
admitting a local morphism into a local object
u:A\rightarrow A_{0}
is itself a local object.

Proof.

If
(n_{i}:A{\longrightarrow}B_{i})_{i\in I}
is a generalized cover of
A
then its pushout along
u
is a generalized cover for
A_{0}
, hence admits a lifting
r
for some
i
, so we have a square that diagonalizes because
u
is local and
n_{i}
is in
\mathcal{V}_{A}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists}

∎

From this we deduce the following, which is a form of admissibility for set-valued models; a first occurence of this result is for instance
[
6
]
[Theorem 3.4.1]

Corollary 1.2.8
.

For any arrow
f:B\rightarrow A
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
with
A
a
J
-local object, the
({\bf Et},{\bf Loc})
factorization of
f

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{f}}

returns a
J
-local object
A_{f}

We have seen in the previous subsection that the factorization structure is inherited by the category of models in any Grothendieck topos. Similarly, let us see now how the admissibility structure itself is inherited, inducing the multireflectivity of the category of local object and local maps at any Grothendieck topos.

Proposition 1.2.9

(
[
21
]
Proposition 3.3.3.1)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a Grothendieck topos and
u:F\rightarrow E
in
{\bf Loc}[\mathcal{E}]
with
E
in
\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{E}]
: then
F
itself is in
\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{E}]
.

This says that for any Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
, the category of
\mathbb{T}
-models in
\mathcal{E}
inherits the admissibility structure defined by the geometry
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)

Corollary 1.2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a Grothendieck topos: then for any
f:F\rightarrow E
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
with
E
in
\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{E}]
, then in the
({\bf Et}[\mathcal{E}],{\bf Loc}[\mathcal{E}])
-factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{f}}

the middle term is in
\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{E}]
.

All of this justifies the terminology for local objects and local transformations as our objects of interest behave locally, at points, as such objects. We also have the following closure property under retracts:

Proposition 1.2.11

(
[
21
]
[Proposition 3.3.3.9] )
.

In any topos
\mathcal{E}
, a retract of a local object still is a local object. Similarly retracts of local maps between local objects still are local, as it can be tested point-wisely.

1.2.12
.

The main result of the construction of spectra is the existence of a certain adjunction at the level of categories of modelled topoi, as we are going to see in the next sections. If we restrict to the categories of models in fixed topoi, then no such adjunction exists (would they exist, there would be no need of constructing spectra); yet we meet an intermediate situation of
multiadjunction
.
Recall briefly that a
right multiadjoint
is a functor
U:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
such that any
B
in
\mathcal{B}
admits a small multi-initial family in the comma
B\downarrow U
, that is, a cone of
local units

(n_{x}:B\rightarrow U(A_{x}))_{x\in I_{B}}
such that any
f:B\rightarrow U(A)
in
\mathcal{B}
admits a unique factorization through a unique
x\in I_{B}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U(A_{x})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(u_{f})}

The local units assume conjointly the universal property of the unit of an adjunction. See
[
9
]
and
[
23
]
for all details on this notion: we would like to emphisize this precise application of the notion of multi-adjunction (established at
[
21
]
[theorem 3.3.3.6]):

Theorem 1.2.13
.

Any a geometry
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)
induces at each Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
a right multi-adjoint

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{E}]^{{\bf Loc}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{J,{\bf Loc}}[\mathcal{E}]}

In general, geometric extensions lack a free construction: for instance there is no universal way to correct a commutative ring into a local ring, for the latter are models of a non-cartesian geometric theory. Geometries are intermediate situations where this failure is somewhat not to bad as one at least has a topos-wise “multi-free” construction in presence of a suited factorization system. As we will see, the purpose of the spectrum is to provide a way to glue altogether those multi-adjunctions into a true global adjunction – provided one is allowed to navigate across topoi to properly gather the cones of local units as the stalks of some sheaf behaving as the free local object. The details of the multi-adjunction aspect of this question are addressed in chapters 2, 3, 4 of
[
21
]
, and will be gathered in an upcomming companion paper.

1.3
Morphisms of geometries

Let us also give a few words on the 2-category of geometries and how they can be compared.

Definition 1.3.1
.

A
transformation of geometries

(\mathbb{T}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{1},J_{1})\rightarrow(\mathbb{T}_{2},\mathcal{V}_{2},J_{2})
consists of a functor
\Phi:\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}
such that

-

\Phi
is lex

-

\Phi(\mathcal{V}_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{2}

-

\Phi
induces a morphism of sites
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{1}},J_{1})\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{2}},J_{2})

Definition 1.3.2
.

In the following we denote
\mathfrak{Geom}
the 2-category whose

-

0-cells are geometries
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)

-

1-cells are transformations of geometries
(\mathbb{T}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{1},J_{1})\rightarrow(\mathbb{T}_{2},\mathcal{V}_{2},J_{2})

-

and 2-cells between two transformations of geometries
\Phi
,
\Psi
are natural transformation between the underlying lex functors
\alpha:\Phi\Rightarrow\Psi
.

Remark 1.3.3
.

Observe that
\Phi:\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}
induces a geometric morphism
\Phi^{*}\dashv\Phi_{*}
(we also denote as
\Phi
) which moreover restricts to the sheaf subtopoi as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}},J_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}},J_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

1.3.4
.

Moreover, since
\Phi
is a morphism of site, any
J_{2}
-continuous lex functor
F:\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is sent by precomposition with
\Phi
to a
J_{1}
-continuous lex functor
\Phi[\mathcal{E}]_{*}F=F\Phi
. Hence the direct image part of the adjunction above restricts to local objects; the same is true regarding local maps: for any Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
, we have
\Phi[\mathcal{E}]_{*}({\bf Loc}_{1}[\mathcal{E}])\subseteq{\bf Loc}_{2}[\mathcal{E}]
. This tel us that any transformation of geometries induces a pseudocommutative square intertwinning the associated right multi-adjoints obtained at
theorem

1.2.13
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}_{J_{2}}[\mathcal{E}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbb{T}_{2}}[\mathcal{E}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}_{J_{1}}[\mathcal{E}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbb{T}_{1}}[\mathcal{E}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi[\mathcal{E}]_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{2}[\mathcal{E}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}[\mathcal{E}]}

In the following, notably the examples, we will prefer to visualize the transformations of geometries through such squares (in particular the version for set-valued models) rather than as lex functors satisfying the condition above, for the evocative virtue of such a representation.

1.4
\mathbb{T}
-Modelled topoi

1.4.1
.

We have a pseudofunctor, which is representable by
\mathcal{S}[\mathbb{T}]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf GTop}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Cat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}[-]}

and is defined as follows:

-

for 0-cells, it returns the category
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
of
\mathbb{T}
-models

-

for a 1-cell
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, it returns the inverse image functor

\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{F}]

which is moreover lex and cocontinuous, being left adjoint to the direct image functor

\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{F}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]

-

on a 2-cell
\alpha:f\Rightarrow g
it returns the natural transformation also denoted as
\alpha

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{F}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

This defines a 2-fibration with 1-truncated fibers

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int\mathbb{T}[-]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf GTop}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbb{T}}}

This bicategory can be considered as a category of all models of
\mathbb{T}
regardless of their base topos. An object in this category is a
modelled topos
, that is, a pair
(\mathcal{E},E)
with
\mathcal{E}
a Grothendieck topos and
E
in
\mathbb{T}[E]
. However in the following, we choose to work with an
algebraic convention
in the sense that we want morphisms between modelled topoi to have the orientation of the morphisms of models rather than the orientation of the underlying geometric morphism. To this end, we use the following, which is nothing but the
direct fibration
associated to
\mathbb{T}[-]
:

Definition 1.4.2
.

The bicategory
\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}
of
\mathbb{T}
-modelled topoi has

-

for 0-cells, modelled topoi
(\mathcal{E},E)

-

for 1-cells,
(f,\phi):(\mathcal{F},F)\rightarrow(\mathcal{E},E)
with
f:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
a geometric morphism and
\phi
consisting of a pair
(\phi^{\flat},\phi^{\sharp})
with

\phi^{*}F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f^{\flat}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E\quad\textrm{ and }\quad F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi^{\sharp}}}{{\longrightarrow}}f_{*}E

mates along the adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}

-

for 2-cells
\alpha:(f,\phi)\rightarrow(g,\psi)
, 2-cell
\alpha:f\rightarrow g
in
{\bf GTop}
satisfying

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\flat}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\flat}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g_{*}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\sharp}_{E}}

From its construction, this bicategory is equipped with a forgetful functor which is both a fibration and an opfibration
p_{\mathbb{T}}:\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}\rightarrow{\bf GTop}^{{\rm op}}
.

1.4.3
.

Before turning to local objects, it is worth giving some details about the morphisms in this category, which will be shown enlightening in Section 4.3. The 2-category
\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}
inherits from its fibrational and opfibrational properties over
{\bf GTop}^{{\rm op}}
two factorization systems

(Vertical, Cartesian) and (Cocartesian, Vertical)

as seen below in the two alternative factorizations of a same morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F}_{2},f^{*}F_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F}_{1},f_{*}F_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F}_{2},F_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{F_{1}},(\phi^{\sharp},\phi^{\sharp}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,(1_{f^{*}F_{1}},\eta_{F_{1}}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{F_{2}},(\phi^{\flat},\phi^{\flat}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,(\epsilon_{F_{2}},1_{f_{*}F_{2}}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\phi)}

provided by the respective unit-counit triangles for the
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
-adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}f_{*}F_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\phi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{F_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{*}f^{*}F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{*}F_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}\phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\sharp}}

1.5
\mathbb{T}_{J}
-locally modelled topoi

In the previous chapter, the category of locally modelled topoi was a non-full sub-bicategory of the oplax slice over the classifier of local objects: in fact this can be obtained as the Grothendieck construction associated to a sub-pseudofunctor of the representable.

1.5.1
.

The local data associated to
(\mathcal{V},J)
also define a pseudofunctor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf GTop}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Cat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}_{J}[-]^{{\bf Loc}}}

and again we can consider the associated direct 2-fibration; but for each topos
\mathcal{E}
, we have a non-full inclusion of category

\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{E}]^{{\bf Loc}[\mathcal{E}]}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]

inducing the following non-full sub-bicategory of
\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}
defined:

Definition 1.5.2
.

The bicategory
\mathbb{T}_{J}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}^{\bf Loc}
of

\mathbb{T}_{J}
-locally modelled topoi
has

-

for 0-cells pairs
(\mathcal{E},E)
with
E
in
\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{E}]
,

-

for 1-cells pairs
(f,\phi):(\mathcal{F},F)\rightarrow(\mathcal{E},E)
with
f:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
and
\phi
is such that
\phi^{\flat}:f^{*}F\rightarrow E
is in
{\bf Loc}[\mathcal{E}]
,

-

and the same 2-cells as
\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}
.

In particular this inclusion is a strict morphism of opfibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}_{J}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}^{\bf Loc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf GTop}^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{J,{\bf Loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{J,{\bf Loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbb{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

so that in the following, we may innocently write
\iota_{J,{\bf Loc}}(\mathcal{E},E)
as
(\mathcal{E},wE)
where we denote abusively
w
for the faithful (but non-full) inclusion
w_{\mathcal{E}}:\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{E}]^{{\bf Loc}[\mathcal{E}]}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
: in other words,
\iota_{J,{\bf Loc}}
just forget the localness of models and arrows without modifying the underlying topos. However beware that
p_{J,{\bf Loc}}
does not inherit the fibration structure of
p_{\mathbb{T}}
as
\mathbb{T}_{J}
models are not stable under direct image of geometric morphisms.

Remark 1.5.3
.

We should really emphasize that the inclusion is not full, for the restriction imposed on the inverse image part of morphism of locally modelled topoi. However it is faithful, and moreover it can be shown to lift equivalences – see
[
21
]
[lemma 6.1.2.3].

The problem of the spectrum is to construct a left bi-adjoint to this inclusion functor – that is, to provide a “free” locally modelled topos under any modelled topos. There are several manners to do so, and in this paper, we will focus on a concrete construction of the spectrum as a locally modelled topos, describing both a site for the underlying topos and the associated local object as a structure sheaf.

In the remaining of this paper, we fix a geometry
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)
and denote as
{\bf Et}
and
{\bf Loc}
the associated factorization system in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, as well as
\mathbb{T}_{J}
the geometric extension of
\mathbb{T}
encoded by
J
.

2
Spectral site of a set-valued model

We process in two times in order to give a detailed insight of the process of the construction. We first emphasize the construction of the spectrum for a set valued model, as well as several geometric properties of the spectra of the different class of objects and maps involved in a geometry. We also want to give a very explicate description of how processes the universal property of the associated structure sheaf, and present a first version of the spectral adjunction for set-valued models which is more akin to what people encounter in algebraic geometry. We also discuss sheaf representability condition, which are an important topic of sheaf theoretic algebra. While the key results of relative to the construction and the adjunction are already present in
[
6
]
and also
[
1
]
, we give here quite different description

2.1
Spectral topology on the etale generator

Recall that for any set-valued
\mathbb{T}
-model
B
we denote as
\mathcal{V}_{B}
the etale generator of
B
, which consists of arrows obtained as pushouts under
B
of finitely presented etale maps in
\mathcal{V}
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

For any
B
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, the opposite category of the etale generator
\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}
can be equipped with the
spectral pretopology
, which is the Grothendieck pretopology
J_{B}
defined from the duals of the families

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\Bigg)_{i\in I}

such that there exists some arrow
b:K\rightarrow C
and a covering family
(l_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
dual of a covering family in
J
such that for each
i\in I
we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Remark 2.1.2
.

In particular, in the case of a finitely presented object
K
, we know
\mathcal{V}_{K}
to be made of finitely presented etale arrows in
\mathcal{V}
, that is,
\mathcal{V}_{K}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}
for
\mathcal{V}
is closed under pushouts between finitely presented objects. Then, in particular, any map
n
in
\mathcal{V}_{K}
has a finitely presented codomain. But now, a covering family in
J_{K}(n)
is induced by pushout from some
(n_{i}:K_{0}\rightarrow K^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
corresponding to a covering family in
J
as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

But
J
, as a Grothendieck coverage, is closed under pullback, and hence the pushout family
(m_{i}:K^{\prime}\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
is dual to a covering family in
J(K^{\prime})
. Conversely, any family
(m_{i}:K^{\prime}\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
dual to a cover of
K^{\prime}
in
J
induces trivially a covering family of
l
in
J_{K}
as it is a pushout of itsefl along the identity
1_{K^{\prime}}
. Hence a familly
(m_{i}:n\rightarrow n_{i})_{i\in I}
is covering in
J_{K}
if and only if
(m_{i}:{\rm cod}(n)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n_{i}))_{i\in I}
is dual to a covering family in
J({\rm cod}(n))
.

Definition 2.1.3
.

For a set-valued model
B
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
,
(\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}},J_{B})
is called the
spectral site
of
B
. Then define the
(Coste) spectrum
of
B
as

{\bf Spec}(B)={\bf Sh}(\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}},J_{B})

We have a geometric embedding into the presheaf topos over
\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Remark 2.1.4
.

Observe that the sheafification functor
\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}
extends into a functor between categories of
\mathbb{T}
-models and
\mathbb{T}_{J}
-models. In fact we have a pseudocommutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(B)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}_{J}[\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}_{J}[{\bf Spec}(B)]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

which is the pseudonaturality square of the natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Geom}[\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}},-]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Geom}[{\bf Spec}(B),-]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Geom}[\iota_{B},-]}

at the inclusion
\iota_{J}:\mathcal{S}[\mathbb{T}_{J}]\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}[\mathbb{T}]
.

2.1.5
.

Now we turn to the functoriality of the construction. For a morphism
f:B_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, the geometric morphism
{\bf Spec}(f)
is computed from the pushout functor along
f

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{B_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{B_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}

sending a finitely presented etale arrow to its pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(f_{*}n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{*}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

But now, observe that the pushout functor sends finite colimits of
\mathcal{V}_{B_{1}}
to finite colimits in
\mathcal{V}_{B_{2}}
hence defines a lex functor
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B_{2}}
. Moreover, this functor is
J_{B_{1}}
-continuous, by composition of pushouts. Hence Diaconescu applies and returns an extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{B_{1}}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{B_{2}}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(B_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(B_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B_{2}}}\text{\char 232\relax}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B_{1}}}\text{\char 232\relax}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lan}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B_{1}}}\text{\char 232\relax}}\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B_{2}}}\text{\char 232\relax}f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

which is the inverse image part of
{\bf Spec}(f)
.

2.2
Etale maps produce (pro-)etale geometric morphisms

2.2.1
.

\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}
is a lex site coding for “basic compact open inclusions”. Objects of the sheaf topos
{\bf Spec}(B)\hookrightarrow[\mathcal{V}_{B},\mathcal{S}]
should be seen as generalized opens of the spectral topology, while objects of
{\rm Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{B})
, which are arbitrary etale arrows under
B
, should be seen as saturated compacts of the spectral topology. In particular, the embedding
\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}\hookrightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
exibits
\mathcal{V}_{B}
as a basis of
basic compact sets that are open
– and
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}
as a basis of
open sets that are compacts
.

The following observation (already present in
[
1
]
[proposition 38] motivates the name for etale arrows:

Proposition 2.2.2
.

Finitely presented etale arrows
n:B\rightarrow C
under
B
correspond to etale geometric morphisms of the form:

{\bf Spec}(C)\simeq{\bf Spec}(B)/\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\bf Spec}(n)}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\bf Spec}(B)

Proof.

For
n:B\rightarrow C
in
\mathcal{V}_{B}
we have an equivalence of categories

\mathcal{V}_{C}\simeq n\downarrow\mathcal{V}_{B}

sending
m:C\rightarrow D
to the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces mn}

and conversely any triangle
l=mn
in
n\downarrow\mathcal{V}_{B}
to the underlying arrow
m
. In particular we have

\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{C}^{{\rm op}}}\simeq\widehat{(n\downarrow\mathcal{V}_{B})^{{\rm op}}}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}/\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}

But also by the expression of slices in a sheaf category (see
[
24
]
[III, Proposition 5.4]), we know that the topology induced on
n\downarrow\mathcal{V}_{B}
by
J_{B}
is the same as
J_{C}
– this is the corresponding topology
J^{\prime}
corresponding to
J_{C}
through the equivalence above – and we have

{\bf Spec}(C)\simeq{\bf Sh}(n\downarrow\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B},J^{\prime})\simeq{\bf Spec}(B)/\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}

∎

Remark 2.2.3
.

Observe that we have a 2-pullback square in the bicategory of Grothendieck topoi

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(B)/\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}/\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

exhibiting the etale geometric morphism
{\bf Spec}(n)
as the 2-pullback of the etale geometric morphism associated to
{\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}}
.

Remark 2.2.4
.

The further left adjoint of the inverse image will be induced from the postcomposition functor
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}
sending
m:C\rightarrow D
to the composite
mn:B\rightarrow D
which is in
\mathcal{V}_{B}
. This functor defines a left adjoint

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{C}^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The intuition that objects of
\mathcal{V}_{B}
are compact can be formalized thanks to the following property. Recall that a geometric morphism is said to be
tidy
if its direct image part preserves filtered colimits. From
[
20
]
[Theorem 4.8] we know that tidy geometric morphisms are stable under 2-pullback.

Proposition 2.2.5
.

For
n:B\rightarrow C
in
\mathcal{V}_{B}
, the geometric morphism
{\bf Spec}(n):{\bf Spec}(C)\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
is tidy.

Proof.

Recall we can express
{\bf Spec}(n)
as the pullback of the etale geometric morphism
\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}/\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}
along
\iota_{B}
. But we know that
\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}
is a finitely presented object in the presheaf topos
\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}
, so that the associated internal hom functor
(-)^{\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}}
preserves filtered colimits: hence its associated etale geometric morphism is tidy, and hence its pullback
{\bf Spec}(n)
also is.
∎

Remark 2.2.6
.

Arbitrary etale arrows are not in the topos
{\bf Spec}(B)
, but rather from the side of points and saturated compacts. Hence they do not correspond to etale geometric morphisms in general. In fact observe that an arbitrary etale map
l:B\rightarrow C
is an object of
{\rm Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{B})
, for the factorization system
({\bf Et},{\bf Loc})
was left generated from
\mathcal{V}
; but we have
{\rm Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{B})\simeq{\rm Pro}(\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}})^{{\rm op}}
, which is the pro-completion of
\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}
, whose objects are those functors
\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
which are cofiltered limits of representables: this mimics the fact that arbitrary etale maps are constructed as cofiltered intersection of basic open compact sets. For this reason,
[
1
]
says
pro-etale
for what we call arbitrary etale, reserving “etale” for the basic ones. This can be formalized into the following result:

Theorem 2.2.7
.

Let
l:B\rightarrow C
be an arbitrary etale arrow under
B
. Then
{\bf Spec}(C)
decomposes as a cofiltered pseudolimit

{\bf Spec}(C)\simeq\underset{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}{{\textup{bilim}}}{\bf Spec}({\rm cod}(n))

Proof.

From
[
21
]
[Theorem 1.1.4.3], we know that

\mathcal{V}_{C}\simeq\underset{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\mathcal{V}_{{\rm cod}(n)}

Now recall that for each
(n,a)
in
\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}
, the opposite category of the etale generator
\mathcal{V}_{{\rm cod}(n)}
can be equipped with a pretopology
J_{{\rm cod}(n)}
, and the opposite category of the pseudocolimit

({{\textup{pscolim}}}_{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}\mathcal{V}_{{\rm cod}(n)})^{{\rm op}}

can be equipped with the coarsest topology

\langle\bigcup_{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}q_{(n,a)}(J_{{\rm cod}(n)})\rangle

making the canonical inclusion continuous

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{{\rm cod}(n)}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({{\textup{pscolim}}}_{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}\mathcal{V}_{{\rm cod}(n)})^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{(n,a)}^{{\rm op}}}

From
[
24
]
and
[
12
]
, and also the general version of
proposition

3.2.10
on cofiltered pseudolimits of Grothendieck topoi, we know that the corresponding sheaf topos is the pseudolimit

{\bf Sh}\big(({{\textup{pscolim}}}_{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}\mathcal{V}_{{\rm cod}(n)})^{{\rm op}},\langle\bigcup_{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}q_{(n,a)}(J_{{\rm cod}(n)})\rangle\big)\simeq\underset{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}{{\textup{bilim}}}{\bf Spec}({\rm cod}(n))

and moreover, this topology is exactly the image of the induced topology on the pseudocolimit along the equivalence of categories above with
\mathcal{V}_{C}
.
Now we can also glue the image of the
J_{{\rm cod}(n)}
-covering families along the pushout functors
a_{*}
to generate a topology on
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{C}

\langle\bigcup_{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}a_{*}(J_{{\rm cod}(n)})\rangle

We must prove that any covering family
a_{*}(J_{{\rm cod}(n)})
is covering in
J_{C}
, and conversely that any
J_{C}
covering family is covering in the topology induced from the colimit.

First, let us prove that a cover in the jointly generated pretopology is covering in
J_{C}
. For
(n,a)
in
\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}
,
m
an object of
\mathcal{V}_{{\rm cod}(n)}
and a covering family
(l_{i})_{i\in I}
induced as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

in
J_{{\rm cod}(n)}(m)
, consider the composition of pushouts as in the diagram below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{*}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{*}D_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}l_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then we see that for each
i\in I
the arrow
a_{*}l_{i}
is also the pushout
(m_{*}a\,b)k_{i}
, and this exhibits the pushout family
(m_{*}l_{i})_{i\in I}
as a pushout of a family in
J
, and hence as a covering family of
J_{C}
.

Conversely let us prove that a
J_{C}
-cover is covering in the jointly generated pretopology. For a covering family of some
m
in
\mathcal{V}_{C}
for
J_{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

we know from the essential surjectivity of the equivalence above that
m
is induced as some
a_{*}m^{\prime}
for some
(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Now observe that from the situation below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

there exists by general property of finitely presented objects some
(n_{1},a_{1})
in

and a factorization of

through the intermediate pushout as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

and now we can pushout the family

of

along

in

of the object

in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Moreover, the objects

and

are identified in the pseudocolimit for they are related through an opcartesian morphism, so that

is both covering for the class of
((n,a),m^{\prime})
in the induced topology on the pseudocolimit
{\textup{colim}}_{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}\mathcal{V}_{{\rm cod}(n)}
, and is sent to the covering family
(l_{i})_{i\in I}
of

through the pushout functor

. Hence the

-cover
(l_{i})_{i\in I}
is in the induced topology

, and hence in the jointly generated topology. This proves that the equivalence of categories above induces an equivalence of sites

\big((\underset{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\mathcal{V}_{{\rm cod}(n)})^{{\rm op}},\langle\bigcup_{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}q_{(n,a)}(J_{{\rm cod}(n)}\rangle\big)\simeq\big((\mathcal{V}_{C}^{{\rm op}},J_{C}\big)

so that they induce the same sheaf topos, which proves the desired limit decomposition of
{\bf Spec}(C)
.
∎

Remark 2.2.8
.

In fact one could dispense form this tedious proof and rather announce that this result is an immediate consequence of
theorem

2.7.2
where
{\bf Spec}
is exhibited as a left adjoint, for which it has to send (filtered) colimits in
\mathbb{T}[S]
to filtered colimits in
\mathbb{T}_{J}^{{\bf Loc}}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}
, which can be proved to be computed in
\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}
and have as underlying topos a cofiltered bilimit topos (see
[
21
]
[Propositions 6.2.2.1 and 6.2.2.2]). However we think interesting to remain concrete and dispense to invoke those general statements about bicolimit of modelled topoi which belong to another work.

Remark 2.2.9
.

Observe that the pseudocolimit decomposition of the etale generator induces first a bilimit decomposition of the corresponding presheaf topos:

\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{C}}\simeq\underset{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}/\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}

Since each
{\bf Spec}({\rm cod}(n))
for
(n,a)\in\int\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}
expresses as an etale geometric morphism obtained as a pullback as in
remark

2.2.3
, and pullback commutes with limits, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(n,m)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}{{\textup{bilim}}}{\bf Spec}(B)/\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(n,m)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}/\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

so that we have a pullback in
{\bf GTop}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{C}^{{\rm op}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This means that the natural inclusion
{\bf Spec}(-)\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{(-)}^{{\rm op}}}
is cartesian at etale maps
l\in{\bf Et}
.

2.3
Spectrum and local data

In particular we have the following, for local units are etale arrows (that are seldom finitely presented):

Proposition 2.3.1
.

Points of
{\bf Spec}(B)
correspond to local units
x:B\rightarrow A
.

Proof.

First observe that a point of the spectrum, that is a
J_{B}
-continuous lex functor
x
in
{\bf Lex}[\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}},\mathcal{S}]
is in particular an object of the ind-completion of
{\mathcal{V}_{B}}
, hence an object of
B\downarrow\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, so we can write
x
as an etale arrow
x:B\rightarrow A
; now the condition of continuity says that for a covering
(m_{i}^{{\rm op}}:n_{i}\rightarrow n)_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}
one has

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\underset{i\in I}{\coprod}}\;x(n_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle x(m_{i})\rangle_{i\in I}}

But Yoneda tells us that

\displaystyle x(n)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{B})[\text{\char 136\relax}_{n},x]

\displaystyle=\{l:C\rightarrow A\mid\;ln=x\}

and the surjectivity property above expresses the existence of the dashed arrow in the following diagram for some
i\in I
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

We must prove that
A
is a local object: let
(n_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
be a
J
-cover and
a:K\rightarrow A
. Recall first that
x
, as an etale map, is the filtered colimit of the finitely presented etale maps under
B
over it:
x={\textup{colim}}_{(n,l)\in V_{\mathcal{B}}\downarrow x}
, so is
A={\textup{colim}}_{(n,l)\in V_{\mathcal{B}}\downarrow x}{\rm cod}(n)
. But then for
K
is finitely presented,
a:K\rightarrow A
lift through some
l:C\rightarrow A
as
b:K\rightarrow C
for some factorization of
x
as above. Then we can consider the pushout family
(b_{*}n_{i})_{i\in I}
which is a
J_{B}
-cover of
n
in
\mathcal{V}_{B}
: but then we see in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{*}K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{*}n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

that there must be a dashed arrow as provided by
J_{B}
-injectiveness of
x
, which ensures existence of a lift for
A
. Hence
A
is local and
x
is a local unit. ∎

Remark 2.3.2
.

More generally, geometric morphisms
x:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(F)
will correspond to etale arrows
x:\,!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B\rightarrow E
in
{\bf Et}[\mathcal{E}]
with
E
in
\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{E}]
: however this correspondence can only be seen through the structure sheaf, which will be described later in this section. For now let us focus on the set-valued points.

2.3.3
.

At the level of points, an etale map of finite presentation
n:B\rightarrow C
produces a discrete fibration

{\bf pt}({\bf Spec}(C))\simeq{\bf pt}({\bf Spec}(B)/\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}(\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}))\rightarrow{\bf pt}({\bf Spec}(B))

More generally, this remains true for an arbitrary etale map
l:B\rightarrow C
; cartesian lifts are computed as follows: if
x
is a local unit under
B
and
x^{\prime}
a local unit under
C
, then a morphism
x\rightarrow x^{\prime}l
is a square as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with
f
an arbitrary arrow; but the etale-local factorization of this very map comes equiped with a diagonalization of the square below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime\prime}}

where the diagonal
x^{\prime\prime}:C\rightarrow A_{f}
is a local unit equiped with a lift
x^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow x^{\prime}
given by
u_{f}
. Moreover, as the functor of points
{\bf pt}\simeq{\bf Geom}[\mathcal{S},-]
preserves pseudolimits, we have a pseudolimit of category

{\bf pt}({\bf Spec}(C))\simeq\underset{(n,a)\in\int\!\text{\char 136\relax}_{l}}{{\textup{pslim}}}{\bf pt}({\bf Spec}({\rm cod}(n))

Remark 2.3.4
.

Observe that arbitrary etale maps under
B
correspond to points of the presheaf topos
\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}
as
{\bf Lex}[\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B},\mathcal{S}]\simeq{\rm Ind}(\mathcal{V}_{B})
.

2.3.5
.

As the term “etale” was justified by the fact that finitely presented etale morphisms in
\mathcal{V}_{B}
where sent to etale geometric morphisms by
{\bf Spec}
, the name of “local” for objects is justified as follows. Recall that a geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is said to be
local
if its inverse image part
f^{*}
is full and faithful and moreover the direct image part
f_{*}
has a further right adjoint
f^{!}
. In particular, a Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
is said to be
local
if the global section functor
\Gamma:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
has a further right adjoint – the full-and-faithfulness condition being automatic in this context. For topoi are presentable, the adjoint functors tells us this amounts for
\Gamma
to preserving colimits, or equivalently, since
\Gamma=\mathcal{E}[1_{\mathcal{E}},-]
, for
1_{\mathcal{E}}
to be
tiny
.

Local topoi can be presented through a special class of sites, see
[
16
]
[C3.6.3 (d)]. A site
(\mathcal{C},J)
is said to be
local
if it has a terminal object
1
which is
J
-indecomposable (that is, the only covering sieve for
1
is the maximal sieve). Then for such a site,
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)
is a local topos.

2.3.6
.

The prototypical example of local geometric morphism is the universal domain map
\partial_{0}:\mathcal{E}^{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
of a topos. Now for any point
p:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, we can consider the
Grothendieck Verdier localization at
p
, which is defined as the pseudopullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}_{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Its universal property is that for any Grotendieck topos
\mathcal{F}
, we have an equivalence with the cocomma category

{\bf Geom}[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E}_{p}]\simeq p\;!_{\mathcal{F}}\!\downarrow{\bf Geom}[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E}]

In particular, when
\mathcal{F}
is
\mathcal{S}
, we have an equivalence of categories

{\bf pt}(\mathcal{E}_{p})\simeq p\downarrow{\bf pt}(\mathcal{E})

From
[
17
]
[Theorem 3.7] we know that if
\mathcal{E}
has
(\mathcal{C},J)
as a lex site of definition, then
\mathcal{E}_{p}
can be expressed as a cofiltered pseudolimit of etale geometric morphisms

\mathcal{E}_{p}\simeq\underset{(C,a)\in\int p^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\mathcal{E}/\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 232\relax}_{C}

where
\int p^{*}
is the cofiltered category of elements of the
J
-flat functor
p^{*}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
.

Proposition 2.3.7
.

Let
A
be a local object in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
: then
{\bf Spec}(A)
is a local topos.

Proof.

We prove that the spectral site
(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{A},J_{A})
is local: indeed, for
A
is a local object, for any
J
-cover
(n_{i}:A\rightarrow B_{i})_{i\in I}
the identity
1_{A}
factorizes through some
n_{i}
. Similarly, in
\mathcal{V}_{A}
, any
J_{A}
-cover
(m_{i}:1_{A}\rightarrow n_{i})_{i\in I}
of the initial object
1_{A}
admits a retraction for some
i
so that the corresponding
m_{i}
retracts on
1_{A}
. Hence the dual of
1_{A}
is part of the sieve generated by the cover
(m_{i})_{i\in I}
: but as it is the initial object of
\mathcal{V}_{A}
, this means that any arrow is par of this generated sieve: hence any
J_{A}
-cover of
1_{A}
generates the maximal sieve, which is the only covering sieve for
1_{A}
. Hence
(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{A},J_{A})
is a local site, and
{\bf Spec}(A)
a local topos.
∎

We also have a partial converse of this proposition, allowing to test whether an object is local at the level of its spectrum – though this requires strictly an additional assumption of subcanonicity.

Proposition 2.3.8
.

Suppose that for any object
B
, the spectral topology
J_{B}
is subcanonical. Then an object
A
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
is local if and only if
{\bf Spec}(A)
is a local topos.

Proof.

The direct implication was done in the previous item. The converse relies on subcanonicity. If
J_{A}
is granted as subcanonical, then any corepresentable
\text{\char 232\relax}_{n}
for
n
in
\mathcal{V}_{A}
is actually a sheaf for
J_{A}
. Now if
A
is such that
{\bf Spec}(A)
is local, then the terminal object
1_{{\bf Spec}(A)}
is tiny, and moreover coincides with the representable
\text{\char 232\relax}_{1_{A}}
. As
J_{A}
-covers are sent to colimits in
{\bf Spec}(A)
, any
J_{A}
-cover
(m_{i}:1_{A}\rightarrow n_{i})_{i\in I}
yields a colimit decomposition
\text{\char 232\relax}_{1_{A}}\simeq{\textup{colim}}_{i\in I}\text{\char 232\relax}_{n_{i}}
and this decomposition admits a section
\text{\char 232\relax}_{1_{A}}\rightarrow\text{\char 232\relax}_{n_{i}}
as
\text{\char 136\relax}_{1_{A}}
is tiny. But we supposed
J_{A}
to be subcanonical, so that
\mathcal{V}_{A}^{{\rm op}}\hookrightarrow{\bf Spec}(A)
is full and faithful, so that this sections must come from a unique section
r:n_{i}\rightarrow 1_{A}
of
m_{i}
. This exactly says that
A
lifts any of its extended
J_{A}
-covers, hence is a
J
-local object.
∎

We also have this corollary from
theorem

2.2.7
:

Corollary 2.3.9
.

For
x:B\rightarrow A_{x}
a local unit under
B
, the geometric morphism
{\bf Spec}(x):{\bf Spec}(A_{x})\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
is the Grothendieck-Verdier localization of
{\bf Spec}(B)
at the point
p_{x}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
.

To conclude this section, we characterize the class of geometric morphisms the spectrum sends local maps to. Let us first introduce the following notion, which is also related to
[
3
]
[Section 4.7]:

Definition 2.3.10
.

A geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is said to be
terminally connected
if the inverse image part
f^{*}
lifts global elements, that is, if for any
F
in
\mathcal{F}
one has

\mathcal{E}[1,f^{*}(F)]\simeq\mathcal{F}[1,F]

Lemma 2.3.11
.

Let
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
be a morphism of sites between small lex sites with
(\mathcal{D},K)
a local site. If
f
lifts global elements then
{\bf Sh}(f):{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},J)\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)
is terminally connected. Moreover, if
(\mathcal{C},J)
and
(\mathcal{D},K)
both are subcanonical, the converse also holds.

Proof.

Suppose that
f
lifts global elements, so that we have a natural isomorphism
\mathcal{D}[1_{\mathcal{D}},f]\simeq\mathcal{C}[1_{\mathcal{C}},-]
. We prove this suffices to lift also global elements of
{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}
that arise in
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)
. Let be
a:1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}E
with
E
a object of
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)
. Since
\mathcal{C}
generates
\mathcal{E}
under colimits and
1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}
is tiny by localness of
{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}
, there exists a factorization of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}(x)}

which is moreover essentially unique in the sense that for any two such lifts are identified with
a
in the colimit
{\textup{colim}}_{(C,a)\in\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}\downarrow E}{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)[1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)},{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}]
.

As
{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}
restricts as
f
from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
, one has
{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(C)}

It suffices then to prove one can lifts uniquely global elements of the form
b:1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(C)}
. Beware that such maps do not necessarily comes from
\mathcal{D}
for the topology
K
is not supposed to be subcanonical so
\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}
may not be fully faithful. However, since
K
covers are sent to canonical cover in
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)
, and the terminal object comes from the terminal object of the site
1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{1_{\mathcal{D}}}
, we know that any such global element
b
is induced through the universal property of colimits by the image of a
K
-cover
(u_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow 1_{\mathcal{D}})_{i\in I}
together with a cocone
(v_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow f(C))_{i\in I}
such that for any
i
in
I
one has

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{D_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(C)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{u_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}_{v_{i}}}

But by localness of the site (equivalently, by localness of
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)
), the identity of
1_{\mathcal{D}}
factorizes through some
u_{i}
as
1_{\mathcal{D}}=u_{i}w
in
\mathcal{D}
. This factorization is essentially unique in the sense that for any other factorization through
1_{\mathcal{D}}=u_{i^{\prime}}w^{\prime}
then
w,w^{\prime}
factorize through the pullback
D_{i}\times_{D}D_{i^{\prime}}
and are hence identified in the corresponding colimit in
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)
. Hence we get an element
v_{i}w:1_{\mathcal{D}}\rightarrow f(C)
in
\mathcal{D}
, which comes uniquely from an element
\overline{c}:1_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow C
in
\mathcal{C}
. For
w
is a section of
u_{i}
, we have that
f(c)=b
.

In the case where both sites are subcanonical, that is, if both
\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{K}\text{\char 136\relax}
are fully faithful, assuming
{\bf Sh}(f)
to be terminally connected yields the following isomorphism in each
C
of
\mathcal{C}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[1_{\mathcal{D}},f(C)]

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)[1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)},{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{C})]

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)[1_{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)},\text{\char 136\relax}_{C}]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{C}[1_{\mathcal{C}},C]

∎

Proposition 2.3.12
.

Let
u:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
be a local map between local objects. Then
{\bf Spec}(u):{\bf Spec}(A_{2})\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(A_{1})
is terminally connected.

Proof.

Recall that
{\bf Spec}(u)^{*}
restricts as a site morphism
u_{*}:(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{A_{1}},J_{A_{1}})\rightarrow(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{A_{2}},J_{A_{2}})
given by the pushout functor along
u
. From the proof of
proposition

2.3.7
, we know in particular that
(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{A_{2}},J_{A_{2}})
is a local site. Hence from
lemma

2.3.11
, it suffices to prove
u_{*}
to lift global elements at the level of the spectral sites. Now, suppose one has, for some etale map
n:A_{1}\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
in
\mathcal{V}_{A_{1}}
, a global element of
u_{*}n
, which is the same as a retraction as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(u_{*}n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{*}n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{*}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

so that we have a factorization
u=rn_{*}un
. But then, for
u
is local and
n
is etale, we know by general properties of orthogonal factorization systems that
n
must retract on
1_{A_{1}}
for the etale part of
u
is invertible, which produces a retraction
\overline{r}
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(u_{*}n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{*}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{*}n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r}}

and we have by cancellation of pushout that
u_{*}(\overline{r})=r
; moreover such an
\overline{r}
has to be unique: for any other choice
r^{\prime}
of retraction with
u_{*}(r^{\prime})=r
, one would have

u(r^{\prime})=rn_{*}u=u\overline{r}
and
r^{\prime}n=\overline{r}n
, so that
r^{\prime}
and
\overline{r}
would be simultaneously equalized by
n
and coequalized by
u
: but it is a general property of factorization systems that any two arrows that are simultaneously equalized by a left map and coequalized by a right map are actually equal, which enforces that
r^{\prime}=\overline{r}
. Hence
u_{*}
lifts global elements, and by the previous lemma, this prove
{\bf Spec}(u)
is terminally connected.
∎

Remark 2.3.13
.

This proves that, for any geometry
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)
, the functor sending a set-valued
\mathbb{T}
-model in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
to the underlying topos of its spectrum (which we will abusively denote
{\bf Spec}
in this remark) restricts as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{S}]^{{\bf Loc}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textbf{LTop}^{\textbf{TCo}})^{{\rm op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf GTop}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{V},J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}}

where
\textbf{LTop}^{\textbf{TCo}}
denotes the bicategory of local Grothendieck topoi and terminally connected geometric morphisms between them. Moreover, one can show that terminally connected geometric morphisms are left orthogonal in a 2-categorical sense to etale geometric morphisms: this suggests that a kind of “universal” 2-dimensional notion of geometry on the bicategory of Grothendieck topoi is involved in any instance of geometry. This will be investigated in a future work.

2.4
Gros versus petit spectrum
