In this subsection we describe standard sites associated to the different generic classifiers constructed in the previous sections. The techniques used here allow to construct such classifiers of arrows, arrows toward local objects, etale arrows and local forms, but
before
fixing their domain; the specification of the domain is achieved as a variation of this construction and begets the spectrum as described in the previous section. Here the sites are constructed from the arrow category, the etale generator and convenient topologies on them derived from the Grothendieck pretopology provided by a choice of geometry.

2.4.1
.

Before embarking into those constructions, let us give a word on the ambivalence of some limits in
{\bf Lex}
. It was observed first in
[
5
]
that in
{\bf Lex}
pseudopowers coincide with bitensors, so that in fact

(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}})^{2}\simeq\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}}\otimes 2

This oddity comes from the fact that the power projections come equiped with retractions, provided by the identity arrow, which behave as tensor inclusions. Though we will not make use of it, let us also recall here that similarly, pseudoproducts in
{\bf Lex}
are also bicoproducts, and that the pseudoterminal object
1
is also bi-initial. Those properties generalize similar statements about the category of
\wedge
-semilattices.

2.4.2
.

Recall that
(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}})^{2}
is the syntactic site of the theory of morphisms of
\mathbb{T}
-models. We have indeed that

\displaystyle{\bf pt}(\widehat{(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}}})

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf Lex}[(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}},\mathcal{S}]

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Ind}(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]^{2}

From the previous remark the equivalence above generalizes to arbitrary Grothendieck topoi as

\displaystyle{\bf GTop}[\mathcal{E},\widehat{(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}})^{2}]}

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf Lex}[(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}})^{2},\mathcal{E}]

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf Lex}[\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}}\otimes 2,\mathcal{E}]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]^{2}

This exhibits
\widehat{(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}})^{2}}
as the bipower
\mathcal{S}[\mathbb{T}]^{2}
in
{\bf GTop}
, that is, as the classifier of morphisms between
\mathbb{T}
-models.

2.4.3
.

Similarly, from the factorization system
({\bf Et},{\bf Loc})
was left generated from
\mathcal{V}
, we have

\displaystyle{\bf Et}

\displaystyle={\rm Ind}(\mathcal{V})

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf Lex}[\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}},\mathcal{S}]

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf pt}(\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}})

so that
\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}}
is the classifier of etale maps
\mathcal{S}[{\bf Et}]
.

Moreover, the factorization can be constructed as follows from a site theoretic point of view. As
\mathcal{V}
is closed in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2}
under finite colimits, the inclusion
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}}
is lex. Hence for a lex functor
\ulcorner n\urcorner:\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
coding for an etale map in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
, the left Kan extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner n\urcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lan}}_{\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}}\ulcorner n\urcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

is the name of
\iota_{{\bf Et}}(n)
, that is, of the image of
n
along the inclusion
{\bf Et}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]^{2}
. On the other hand, for a lex functor
f:(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
coding for an arbitrary map, then its restriction along
\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}
codes for the etale part of its factorization, that is

\ulcorner f\urcorner\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}=\ulcorner n_{f}\urcorner

while the counit of
\ulcorner f\urcorner

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner f\urcorner\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner f\urcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lan}}_{\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}}\ulcorner f\urcorner\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\ulcorner f\urcorner}}

is the name of the local part, that is

\epsilon_{\ulcorner f\urcorner}=u_{f}

which is obtained by whiskering
\epsilon_{\ulcorner f\urcorner}
with the morphism of site provided by the identity functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}},J^{2})}}
id

Remark 2.4.4
.

Observe that the full faithfulness of
\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}
ensures that
\ulcorner n\urcorner\simeq{\textup{lan}}_{\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}}\ulcorner n\urcorner\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}
, that is, that an etale map is its own etale part.

2.4.5
.

Now, we can equip
(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}})^{2}
with the following pretopology
J^{2}
: for an arrow
k:K\rightarrow K^{\prime}
, a covering family for
J^{2}(k)
is a family of squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{i}}

such that
(n_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
is in
J(K)
. Then observe that
(n^{\prime}_{i}:K^{\prime}\rightarrow K^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
is automatically covering by the stability axiom of pretopologies. In particular one can choose the squares in a family in
J^{2}
to be the pushouts squares along
k
of a covering family
(n_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
in
J(K)
.

Then the adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

defines an adjunction between morphisms of sites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}},J^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},J)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

as
id
sends
J
-covers to
J^{2}
-covers.

Proposition 2.4.6
.

The topos
{\bf Sh}((\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}})^{2},J^{2})
classifies morphisms between
\mathbb{T}_{J}
-models.

2.4.7
.

Now we can also construct an intermediate topology in such a way that the associated sheaf topos classifies maps whose codomain is local – and the domain arbitrary. Define the pretopology
J_{{\rm cod}}
on
(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}}
whose families consist of those squares as above, but where only the codomain family
(n^{\prime}_{i}:K^{\prime}\rightarrow K^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
are supposed to be
J
-covering.

Then in the adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where
!_{(-)}
sends
K
to the initial map
!_{K}:0\rightarrow K
– as 0 is always finitely presented – defines an adjunction of morphisms of sites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}},J_{{\rm cod}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{{\rm op}},J)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{(-)}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

On one hand, the codomain functor sends
J_{{\rm cod}}
families to
J
families by construction; on the other hand, if
(n_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
is in
J(K)
, then the family consisting of those squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{K_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}

is in
J_{{\rm cod}}(!_{K})
.

Proposition 2.4.8
.

The sheaf topos
{\bf Sh}((\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}},J_{{\rm cod}})
classifies morphisms toward
\mathbb{T}_{J}
-models.

Proof.

Let
\ulcorner f\urcorner:(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
be the name of a morphism
f:B\rightarrow A
of
\mathbb{T}
-models in
\mathcal{E}
. Then by Yoneda lemma, requiring it to be
J_{{\rm cod}}
-continuous amounts to saying that for any
k:K\rightarrow K^{\prime}
and any family in
J_{{\rm cod}}(k)
, any square
(a,b):k\rightarrow f
factorizes as below for some
i

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i}}

In particular, for any
(n_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
, we have a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}

so that
A
is actually
J
-local.
∎

2.4.9
.

Now we would need to present the topos classifying local forms, that are, etale maps toward local objects. We saw that
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}
was a site for the classifier of etale maps: then we claim it suffices to restrict the codomain topology to get the classifier of etale forms. As
J
is generated in
\mathcal{V}
, we can consider in
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}
the restricted pretopology
J_{{\rm cod}}\mid_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}}
consisting of squares of finitely presented maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}^{\prime}}

such that the bottom families
(n^{\prime}_{i}:K^{\prime}\rightarrow K^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
are in
J(K^{\prime})
. Beware that
\mathcal{V}
is a full subcategory of
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]^{2}_{\omega}
so that it also contain squares with non-etale top and bottom arrows, but the generation condition makes we actually consider squares entirely made of
\mathcal{V}
maps for the pretopology.

Then the inclusion of the basic etale maps defines a morphism of site

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}},J_{{\rm cod}}\mid_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}},J_{{\rm cod}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}}

Proposition 2.4.10
.

The sheaf topos
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}},J_{{\rm cod}}\mid_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}})
is the classifier of local forms. Moreover we have a local geometric morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}},J_{{\rm cod}}\mid_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}((\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}},J_{{\rm cod}})}}

whose center is the geometric morphism
{\bf Sh}(\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}})
.

Proof.

This is a combination of the previous remarks. A
J_{{\rm cod}}\mid_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}}
-continuous
\ulcorner n\urcorner:\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is the name of an etale map, and from
2.4.3
, we can consider its left Kan extension
{\textup{lan}}_{\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}}\ulcorner n\urcorner
, which is
J_{{\rm cod}}
-continuous as
\iota_{\mathcal{V}}
is a morphism of site: hence its codomain is a local object, so that
\ulcorner n\urcorner
is the name of a local form.

Moreover, the morphism of site
\iota_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}}
happens to be also a comorphism of site: indeed, for any
n:K\rightarrow K^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{V}
and
((u_{i},n_{i}^{\prime}):n\rightarrow k_{i})_{i\in I}
a cover in
J_{{\rm cod}}(\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}(n)
– with, beware,
u_{i}
and
k_{i}
arbitrary finitely presented maps – we have that
(n^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
is a family of etale maps so each composite
n_{i}^{\prime}n
is an etale map and hence the family consisting of the squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{i}n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{i}}

is a
J_{{\rm cod}}\mid_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}}
-cover in
\mathcal{V}
, and we have a factorization at each
i

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{i}n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i}}

ensuring that the image of this cover along
\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}
refines
(u_{i},n^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
: hence
\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}
has the cover lifting property, and is a comorphism of site. Hence, being also continuous, it defines from
[
3
]
[Theorem 7.20, (iii)] a local geometric morphism consisting of a triple of adjoints

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}},J_{{\rm cod}}\mid_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}((\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}},J_{{\rm cod}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}})^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}})_{*}}

∎

Remark 2.4.11
.

Observe that admissibility can be detected at this level of description by combining the previous lemma with
2.4.3
. Indeed, suppose that
\ulcorner f\urcorner:(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}^{2})^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is
J_{\rm cod}
continuous. Then its precomposition along the morphism of site
\iota_{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm op}}
, which codes for its etale maps, is also
J_{{\rm cod}}\mid_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}}
-continuous: this means that the etale part of the factorization of a morphism with local codomain still has a local codomain.

2.4.12
.

We now specify how those data specialize when we fix the domain of morphisms we want to classify: we recover the spectrum together with a coarser object classifying more generally arbitrary maps under the fixed object toward local objects.

In a similar manner of what was established for the category of etale maps under a fixed object, it is proven in
[
22
]
[theorem 2.12] that the category
B\downarrow\mathcal{B}
has as generator of finitely presented objects the class
\mathcal{G}_{B}
of all maps under
B
induced as pushouts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

Here we do not require the maps
k
to be in any specific class beside being finitely presented.
Then as like as the etale generator under
B
is closed under finite colimits and generates left maps under
B
, the class
\mathcal{G}_{B}
is closed under finite colimits in
B\downarrow\mathcal{B}
and generates the coslice under
B
as its ind-completion

\displaystyle B\downarrow\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Ind}(\mathcal{G}_{B})

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf Lex}[\mathcal{G}_{B}^{{\rm op}},\mathcal{S}]

There is a full inclusion
\mathcal{V}_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{B}
, where the fullness comes from right cancellation of etale maps. The spectral topology
J_{B}
that was induced from
J
on the etale generator actually extends to the coslice generator
\mathcal{G}_{B}^{{\rm op}}
. We hence have an inclusion of sites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}},J_{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{G}_{B}^{{\rm op}},J_{B})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}}

which is obviously continous: but it has a stronger property induced from the axioms of a geometry, whose consequence is the following fact (already observed in
[
1
]
[Proposition 39], also related to
[
24
]
[4.10.6] and
[
11
]
):

Theorem 2.4.13
.

For any
\mathbb{T}
-model there is a local geometric morphism over the spectrum of
B

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{G}_{B}^{{\rm op}},J_{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}}

Proof.

The idea is very close to
proposition

2.4.10
: the idea is that
\iota_{B}
is not a mere morphisms of site but simultaneously a comorphism of site, and when working in coslices, this becomes almost automatic: indeed, if one take a
J_{B}
-cover of the image of a finitely presented etale map
\iota_{B}(n)
in
\mathcal{G}_{B}
, as the
J_{B}
-covering families in
\mathcal{G}_{B}
still are induced as pushout of
J
-covers and hence are constituted of etale maps which are closed under composition: then each of the triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}

actually lies in
\mathcal{V}_{B}
and the cover-lifting property is immediate.
∎

2.4.14
.

For this reason, certain sources as
[
1
]
call the category
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{G}_{B},J_{B})
the
gros spectrum
. Beware however it lacks the universal property of the
petit spectrum

{\bf Spec}(B)
! A more abstract reasoning involving bilimits of topoi can help the reader understanding that the gros spectrum, which can be presented as a comma object in
{\bf GTop}
, would only provide a left adjoint only for a restricted category of modelled topoi where the only allowed morphisms
(f,\phi):(\mathcal{E},E)\rightarrow(\mathcal{F},F)
are those for which
\phi^{\flat}
is an isomorphism. This is because the gros spectrum is a spectrum for a
rigid geometry
where all maps are etale, and all local maps are isomorphisms. However, for those considerations are related to another way of presenting the spectrum we chose not to give further details about this fact and now turn to the structure sheaf of the petit spectrum which confers it its universal property.

2.5
The structure sheaf

Now we turn to the structure sheaf, which is obtained from the codomain functor modulo sheafification. It will play the role of the free local object in the spectrum – which will be visualizable at its stalks.

Definition 2.5.1
.

For a set-valued model
B
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, the
structure sheaf
of
B
is the sheaf of set-valued
\mathbb{T}
-models
\widetilde{B}
obtained as

\widetilde{B}=\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}{\rm cod}

2.5.2
.

The structure sheaf can also be described as follows: recall that any
\mathbb{T}
-model
B
is in
{\rm Ind}(\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega})\simeq{\bf Lex}[\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}},\mathcal{S}]
. Moreover, we can consider the
conerve
of the codomain functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[B,{\rm cod}\big]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}_{*}}

which can be composed along the embedding
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]^{{\rm op}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

to produce a lex functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\overline{x},\phi\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[K_{\phi},{\rm cod}\big]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}_{*}}

which we can now compose with the lex localization
\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
to get a lex functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\overline{x},\phi\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[K_{\phi},{\rm cod}\big]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{B}}

Proposition 2.5.3
.

The structure sheaf
\widetilde{B}
is in
\mathbb{T}_{J}[{\bf Spec}(B)]
. In particular for any point
x:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
the stalk
x^{*}\widetilde{B}
is in
\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{S}]
.

Proof.

We have to prove that for any
J
-cover
(\theta_{i}:\{\overline{x}_{i},\phi_{i}\}\rightarrow\{\overline{x},\phi\})_{i\in I}
we have an epimorphism in the category of sheaves
{\bf Spec}(B)={\bf Sh}(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B},J_{B})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{\displaystyle\coprod}\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[K_{\phi_{i}},{\rm cod}\big]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[K_{\phi},{\rm cod}\big]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[f_{\theta_{i}},{\rm cod}\big]\rangle_{i\in I}}

But proving some morphism to be epic in a sheaf topos is a local condition, which however here would be made impossible to test because of the expression of the sheafification. We are in fact going to prove that before sheafification, the map
\langle\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[f_{\theta_{i}},{\rm cod}\big]\rangle_{i\in I}
is a
local epimorphism
, that is, a map that is sent to an epimorphism after sheafification – though not yet being itself an epimorphism in the presheaf category. For sheafification is a left adjoint, it preserves coproducts, that is

\underset{i\in I}{\coprod}\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[K_{\phi_{i}},{\rm cod}\big]\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\underset{i\in I}{\coprod}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[K_{\phi_{i}},{\rm cod}\big]

and moreover, the localness condition is preserved after sheafification, and makes

\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\langle\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[f_{\theta_{i}},{\rm cod}\big]\rangle_{i\in I}\simeq\langle\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[f_{\theta_{i}},{\rm cod}\big]\rangle_{i\in I}

an epimorphism in
{\bf Spec}(B)
.

Let us prove the localness condition for
\langle\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[f_{\theta_{i}},{\rm cod}\big]\rangle_{i\in I}
, which amounts to it to be a local surjection – see for instance
[
19
]
. Take some
b:K_{\phi}\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
; then one can push the
J
-cover
(f_{\theta_{i}}:K_{\phi}\rightarrow K_{\phi_{i}})_{i\in I}
along
b
so we get a cover of
n
in
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{\phi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{\phi_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{*}K_{\phi_{i}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{*}f_{\theta_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces nb_{*}f_{\theta_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\theta_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then for each
i\in I
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[K_{\phi},b_{*}f_{\theta_{i}}\big](b)

\displaystyle=b_{*}f_{\theta_{i}}\,b

\displaystyle={f_{\theta_{i}}}_{*}b\,f_{\theta_{i}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[f_{\theta_{i}},K_{\phi_{i}}\big]({f_{\theta_{i}}}_{*}b)

which exactly says that the restriction of
b
along each member of the cover has an antecedent along
\langle\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[f_{\theta_{j}},b_{*}K_{\phi_{i}}\big]\rangle_{j\in I}
: hence the natural transformation
\langle\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]\big[f_{\theta_{j}},{\rm cod}\big]\rangle_{j\in I}
is a local epimorphism relative to the Grothendieck topology
J_{B}
, hence its sheafification is an epimorphism in
{\bf Spec}(B)
. ∎

Remark 2.5.4
.

Beware that the structure sheaf
\widetilde{B}=\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}{\rm cod}
needs not to return local objects as values; in particular, whenever the topology generated by
J_{B}
is subcanonical,
\widetilde{B}={\rm cod}
, but the codomains of basic etale arrows have no reason to be local objects. This is because local objects are models of a geometric extension of
\mathbb{T}
, being a model of which is a local notion that does not hold globally. Nevertheless, as we saw above, stalks of the structure sheaf are set-valued local objects; but in general having local objects at stalks may not be sufficient to ensure localness of a sheaf itself.

2.5.5
.

Now we address the functoriality of the spectrum at the level of the structure sheaves. For
f:B_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, we saw in
2.1.5
how
{\bf Spec}(f)
was obtained from the pushout functor
f_{*}:\mathcal{V}_{B_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{B_{2}}
. Now to get the direct image part, the bottom arrows at each
n
in
\mathcal{V}_{B_{1}}
of the pushout square
n_{*}f:{\rm cod}(n)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(f_{*}n)
define altogether a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(f)_{*}{\rm cod}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{f}}

which is sent after sheafification to a morphism in
\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(F_{1})]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(f)_{*}\widetilde{F_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{{J_{B_{1}}}}(\nu_{f})}

so we have to put

\widetilde{f}^{\sharp}=\mathfrak{a}_{{J_{B_{1}}}}(\nu_{f})

while we get automatically from the adjunction
{\bf Spec}(f)
a mate

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(f)^{*}\widetilde{F_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}}

There is also a more concrete way to construct this morphism, which moreover expresses its connection to the (etale, locale factorization), exhibiting it as induced from the local part of precompositions with
f
:

Proposition 2.5.6
.

For each
f:B_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
,
\widetilde{f}^{\flat}
is in
{\bf Loc}[{\bf Spec}(B_{2})]
.

Proof.

Recall that inverse images commute with sheafification, so we can first compute the inverse image of the codomain functor
{\bf Spec}(f)^{*}{\rm cod}
and then apply sheafification to get the inverse image
{\bf Spec}(f)^{*}\widetilde{F_{1}}
. But now the inverse image is computed as a left Kan extension
{\textup{lan}}_{f_{*}}{\rm cod}
, which expresses at each
n
of
\mathcal{V}_{B_{2}}
as the filtered colimit

{\textup{lan}}_{f_{*}}{\rm cod}(n)\simeq\underset{f_{*}\downarrow n}{{\textup{colim}}}\,{\rm cod}(m)

But in fact, by the universal propert of the pushout – which can be summed up in the adjunction
f_{*}\dashv f^{!}
where
f^{!}
is precomposition with
f
, we have an equivalence of categories
f_{*}\downarrow n\simeq\mathcal{V}_{B_{1}}\downarrow nf
. This means that the colimit above can be seen equivalently as ranging over all factorizations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(m)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

while the data of all the
g:{\rm cod}(m)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
induce a natural arrow
(u_{f})_{n}:{\textup{lan}}_{f_{*}}{\rm cod}(n)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
. But we saw in
proposition

1.1.5
that this is exactly how the (etale, locale) factorization of the composite
nf:B_{1}\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
is computed as the colimit of all its factorizations through finitely presented etale maps under
B_{1}
, which are precisely the objects of the spectral site of
B_{1}
. Hence in the factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{lan}}_{f_{*}}{\rm cod}(n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{colim}}\,\mathcal{V}_{B_{1}}\downarrow nf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u_{f})_{n}}

{\textup{colim}}\,\mathcal{V}_{B_{1}}\downarrow nf
is etale while
(u_{f})_{n}
is local. Moreover from naturality of this process, this defines a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(f)^{*}{\rm cod}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{f}}

whose sheafification for
J_{F_{2}}
is the desired inverse image part
\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}=\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B_{2}}}(u_{f})
: since sheafification preserves localness, this exhibits
\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}
as a local map.
∎

2.6
The generic etale map and classifying properties of the spectrum

This section is actually the doorstep of the spectral adjunction: here are described the classifying property of the spectrum – where the structure sheaf and a certain canonical map are crucially involved – from which the later adjunction will appear as a corollary.

2.6.1
.

The structure sheaf comes associated with a canonical morphism of sheaves whose component indexes basic etale arrows under the object. For
B
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, the spectrum of
B
has a terminal geometric morphism
!_{{\bf Spec}(B)}
to
\mathcal{S}
with the direct image part sending a sheaf
F:\mathcal{V}_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
to its set of global sections
\Gamma(F)=F(1_{B})
and the inverse image
!_{{\bf Spec}(B)}^{*}
sending a set
X
to the
X
-indexed coproduct
\coprod_{X}1_{{\bf Spec}(B)}
. This adjunction lifts to the categories of
\mathbb{T}
-models

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(B)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{{\bf Spec}(B)}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Moreover this adjunction also exists at the level of the presheaf topos
\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}
, and we have
!_{{\bf Spec}(B)}=!_{\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}}\iota_{B}
. Now the identity of
B
defines a map
1_{B}:B\rightarrow B={\rm cod}_{*}(1_{B})
, and the latter object is actually the global section object of the codomain functor: by the version of the adjunction above relative to the presheaf topos, this defines a canonical map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{!_{\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}}^{*}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{B}}

which is sent after sheafification to a composite map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{!_{{\bf Spec}(B)}^{*}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w\widetilde{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\flat}_{B}}

This very map corresponds itself to a comparison map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma w\widetilde{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\sharp}_{B}}

Those two maps are going to be part of the unit of a restricted
{\bf Spec}\dashv\Gamma
adjunction below.

Proposition 2.6.2
.

The map
\eta^{\flat}_{B}
is etale in
\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(B)]

Proof.

The inverse image presheaf
!_{\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}}^{*}B
can also be described as the constant sheaf returning
B
everywhere; hence the map
\nu_{B}
can also be described as the natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}}^{*}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{B}}

whose component at
n
is
n
itself, which is etale: hence
\nu_{B}
is an etale map as it is pointwise etale, and so is its sheafification
\eta_{B}^{\flat}
.
∎

Definition 2.6.3
.

In the following
\nu^{\flat}_{B}
will be called the
generic etale map under
B
, while
\eta^{\flat}_{B}
will be called the
generic local unit under
B
.

The generic etale map gathers all the etale maps under
B
you need to compute the etale-locale factorizations of maps under inverse images of
B
, as you can extract the etale part of any morphism of sheaves from the inverse image of this morphism. Similarly the generic local unit gathers all admissible factorizations under
B
, so that any morphism into a local object will now factorize through this very map: that is how admissible factorizations through different local units will be turned into an ordinary unit-like factorization. We split the process in two steps encoding first the factorization aspects and then the local data – in the same way we did in the fifth chapter.

2.6.4
.

Recall that the presheaf topos
\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}
is endowed with the codomain presheaf
{\rm cod}:\mathcal{V}_{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
together with the canonical etale map
\nu_{B}:\;!_{\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}}^{*}B\rightarrow{\rm cod}_{*}
whose component at
n
is
n
itself. Then any geometric morphism
x:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}
defines an etale map in
\mathcal{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{wx^{*}{\rm cod}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{*}\eta^{\flat}_{B}}

Similarly, any geometric morphism
x:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
transfers the canonical map
\eta^{\flat}_{B}
in
\mathcal{E}
to an etale map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{wx^{*}\widetilde{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{*}\eta^{\flat}_{B}}

where
x^{*}\widetilde{B}
is a local object.

Now we want to describe the converse process: we want to prove that

-

the presheaf topos
\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}
classifies etale maps under
B
;

-

the sheaf topos
{\bf Spec}(B)
classifies local units under
B
.

2.6.5
.

To any morphism of sheaves
\phi^{\flat}:\;!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B\rightarrow E
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
– corresponding to a morphism
\phi^{\sharp}:B\rightarrow\Gamma E
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, we want to associate a geometric morphism
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
. Suppose that
\mathcal{E}
has a standard site of definition
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}},J_{\mathcal{E}})
. Define the functor
x_{\phi}^{*}:\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}}
as sending a finitely presented etale arrow
n:B\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
to the presheaf
x_{\phi}^{*}(n):\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
which associates to each
c
of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
the set of all possible factorizations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma wE}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(!_{c})}

of the composite
E(!_{c})\phi^{\sharp}
(where
!_{c}:c\rightarrow 1_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}}
is the terminal map of
c
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
) through the finitely presented map
n
, and
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
to postcomposition of
a
with
E(s)
.

Lemma 2.6.6
.

For each
\phi
as above, the functor
x_{\phi}^{*}
lands in
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

We must prove that for each
n
in
\mathcal{V}_{B}
, the presheaf
x_{\phi}^{*}(n)
is a sheaf for
J_{\mathcal{E}}
. But this is a consequence of
E
being a sheaf: for a family
(s_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
in
J_{\mathcal{E}}
, we have
E(c)
is the limit of the
E(c_{i})
for the descent diagram for
(s_{i})_{i\in I}
: hence, any matching family
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
with
a_{i}:n\rightarrow E(s_{i})E(!_{c})\phi^{\sharp}
is in particular a family of arrows
a_{i}:{\rm cod}(n)\rightarrow E(c_{i})
satisfying the commutations of the descent diagram, and hence induces uniquely a map
(a_{i})_{i\in I}:n\rightarrow E(c)
.
∎

The functor constructed above is actually involved in the universal factorization of
\phi
.

Lemma 2.6.7
.

For any
\phi
as above,
x^{*}_{\phi}
defines a geometric morphism
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}
.

Proof.

This amounts to proving that
x_{\phi}^{*}
is lex. Recall that
\mathcal{V}_{B}
is closed under finite colimits in
B\downarrow\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
. Moreover, as (finite) limits in sheaf topoi are pointwise, it suffices to prove that for each
c
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
the functor
x_{\phi}^{*}(-)(c):\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
is lex: but in fact
x_{\phi}^{*}(-)(c)
is nothing but the composite of the functor
B\downarrow\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}][-,E(!_{c})\phi^{\sharp}]
, which, as a representable, turns colimits into limits, along the inclusion of
\mathcal{V}_{B}
into the coslice
B\downarrow\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
. Hence
x_{\phi}^{*}
is lex.

∎

In fact, this is because
x^{*}_{\phi}
“points” to the
etale part
of
\phi
as classified by the presheaf topos
\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{B}^{{\rm op}}}
. Let us precise this intuition – for which the geometric morphism
x_{\phi}
will be called the
classifying morphism
of
\phi
in the following.

Theorem 2.6.8
.

For any morphism of the form
\phi^{\flat}:\;!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B\rightarrow E
in a Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
we have a universal factorization as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{\phi}^{*}{\rm cod}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{\phi}^{*}(\nu^{\flat}_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{\phi^{\flat}}}

with
u_{\phi^{\flat}}
a local map.

Proof.

Our strategy is to construct first a pointwise factorization for the underlying presheaves and prove it coincides with inverse image – forgetting first that
x_{\phi}^{*}
land in the sheaf topos
\mathcal{E}
.
We have at any object
c
of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
a lex functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{\phi}^{*}(-)(c)}

Its category of elements
\int x_{\phi}^{*}(-)(c)
is hence cofiltered and is also the category of all factorizations of the composite
E(!_{c})x^{\sharp}
through an etale arrow on the left: but recall this is the category indexing the filtered colimit from which we constructed the etale part of the etale-local factorization ! In other words, we have

n_{E(!_{c})\phi^{\sharp}}\simeq\underset{(n,a)\in(\int x_{\phi}^{*}(-)(c))^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,n

But the latter coincides also with the expression of the inverse image (in the presheaf topos)
x_{\phi}^{*}{\rm cod}
, which is the left Kan extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}})^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{\phi}^{*})^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lan}}_{(x_{\phi}^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

whose computation at each
c
returns

\displaystyle{\textup{lan}}_{(x_{\phi}^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{\rm cod}(c)

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{(n,a)\in(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}\downarrow x_{\phi}^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,{\rm cod}(n)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm cod}\underset{(n,a)\in(\int x_{\phi}^{*}(-)(c))^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,n

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm cod}(n_{E(!_{c})\phi^{\sharp}})

In other words, the following square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{lan}}_{(x_{\phi}^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{\rm cod}(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(!_{c})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{(n,a)\in(\int x_{\phi}^{*}(c))^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle a\rangle_{(n,a)\in(\int x_{\phi}^{*}(c))^{{\rm op}}}}

coincides with the etale-locale factorization of
E(!_{c})\phi^{\sharp}
, whose local part is the induced map
u_{E(!_{c})\phi^{\sharp}}=\langle a\rangle_{(n,a)\in(\int x_{\phi}^{*}(c))^{{\rm op}}}
.

Since the factorization of presheaves morphisms is pointwise, this exactly says that in
\mathbb{T}[\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}}]
the etale-locale factorization is given as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{lan}}_{(x_{\phi}^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{\rm cod}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lan}}_{(x_{\phi}^{*})^{{\rm op}}}\nu_{B}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{\phi^{\flat}}}

Then the desired factorization in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
is obtained by the sheafification
\mathfrak{a}_{J_{\mathcal{E}}}
, which preserves the etale-local factorization, and turns the left Kan extension into the inverse image functor
x_{\phi}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{J_{\mathcal{E}}}{\textup{lan}}_{(x^{*})^{{\rm op}}}
.
∎

Proposition 2.6.9
.

Let
\phi
be as above, and such that moreover
E
is a local object in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
. Then
x_{\phi}^{*}
defines a geometric morphism
x_{\phi}:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
.

Proof.

J_{B}
-continuity of
x^{*}_{\phi}
results from localness of
E
: let
(m_{i}:n\rightarrow n_{i})_{i\in I}
be a
J_{B}
-cover in
\mathcal{V}_{B}
induced from some
J
-cover
(k_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
along some map
b:K\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
. We have to prove that the following morphism in
\mathcal{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{\displaystyle{\coprod}}x_{\phi}^{*}n_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{\phi}^{*}n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle x_{\phi}^{*}m_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}}

is an epimorphism in
\mathcal{E}
, that is, a local surjection for
J_{\mathcal{E}}
. Let
c
be in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
and
a
in
x_{\phi}^{*}n(c)
: then the composite
ab:K\rightarrow E(c)
is also an object of
E(K)(c)
for
E
seen as a
J
-continuous functor
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, so that
\langle E(m_{i})\rangle_{i\in I}
is itself a local surjection: hence there is a
J_{\mathcal{E}}
-cover
(s_{j}:c_{j}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I^{\prime}}
such that for any
j\in I^{\prime}
,
E(s_{j})(K)(b)
comes from some
E(c_{j})(K_{i})
for some
i\in I
: but this exactly says that for each
j
there is a
i
together with factorization
d
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma wE}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(s_{j})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(!_{c})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(s_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(!_{c_{i}})}

and such a
d
is in particular an element of
x_{\phi}^{*}(n_{i})(c_{j})
such that
x_{\phi}^{*}(n)(s_{i})(a)=x_{\phi}^{*}(m_{i})(c_{j})(b)
. Whence continuity of
x_{\phi}^{*}
.
∎

Corollary 2.6.10
.

For
\phi
as above with
E
a local object, the universal factorization obtained at
theorem

2.6.8
coincides with

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{\phi}^{*}w\widetilde{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{\phi}^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{\phi^{\flat}}}

Proof.

This is because the inverse image part
x_{\phi}^{*}
factorizes through the sheaf topos
{\bf Spec}(B)\simeq{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}},J_{B})
via the sheafification functor
\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}
: hence doing the inverse image along
x_{\phi}^{*}
localizes the sheafification map
\gamma_{B}
, whence

\displaystyle x^{*}_{\phi}{\rm cod}

\displaystyle\simeq x^{*}_{\phi}\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}{\rm cod}

\displaystyle\simeq x^{*}_{\phi}w\widetilde{B}

and the same for the generic etale map.
∎

Remark 2.6.11
.

This corollary is in fact a manifestation of admissibility: it says that the universal factorization of a map into a local object has its middle term local itself, which, in this case, says that the universal factorization above identifies the codomain functor with the structure sheaf, or that in other terms, the classifying morphism
x^{*}_{\phi}
“sees” the codomain functor as a local object.

Now
proposition

2.3.1
generalizes to the following more universal form, where points are replaced with arbitrary geometric morphisms and local units are considered up to inverse images:

Theorem 2.6.12
.

For
B
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
and any Grothendieck topos, geometric morphisms
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
correspond to etale maps
!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B\rightarrow E
with
E
a local object in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
.

Proof.

We saw above how one can toggle between geometric morphisms into the spectrum and local units. We must prove that this correspondence is actually an equivalence of categories.

In one direction, consider a local unit
\phi^{\flat}:\;!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B\rightarrow E
with
E
local. Take its classifying morphism
x_{\phi}:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
and then the inverse image of the generic etale map: this is exactly the left part of the factorization of
\phi^{\flat}
: but hence, if
\phi^{\flat}
was already etale, then its etale part is an isomorphism, exhibiting

\phi^{\flat}\simeq x_{\phi}^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B})\hskip 28.45274ptE\simeq x_{\phi}^{*}w\widetilde{B}

In the other direction, take a geometric morphism
x:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
, then the induced local unit
x^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B})
, and then back the classifying morphism of the later
x^{*}_{x^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B})}:\mathcal{V}^{\rm op}_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
. We must prove that for any
n
in
\mathcal{V}^{\rm op}_{B}
we have an isomorphism of sheaves
x^{*}_{x^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B})}(n)\simeq x^{*}(n)
, which amounts to natural bijections
x^{*}_{x^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B})}(n)(c)\simeq x^{*}(n)(c)
for each
c
of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
. Recall that, on one hand,
x^{*}_{x^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B})}(n)(c)
is the set of all factorizations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma x^{*}w\widetilde{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{*}w\widetilde{B}(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{*}w\widetilde{B}(!_{c})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B}))^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

while on the other hand
x^{*}(n)(c)
is by Yoneda lemma the set of all arrows
\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}\rightarrow x^{*}(n)
in
\mathcal{E}
.
Since
x
defined a morphism in
{\bf Spec}(B)
, its inverse image
x^{*}
factorizes through the sheafification
\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}
, so that we had a natural isomorphism between inverse images
x^{*}w\widetilde{B}\simeq x^{*}{\rm cod}
. The latter is the
J_{\mathcal{E}}
-sheafification of the left Kan extension
{\textup{lan}}_{(x^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{\rm cod}
, whose value at
c
is computed as the filtered colimit
{\textup{lan}}_{(x_{\phi}^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{\rm cod}(c)\simeq{{\textup{colim}}}_{(n,a)\in\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}\downarrow x_{\phi}^{*}}{\rm cod}(n)
: but the indexing set of this colimit is precisely
x^{*}(n)(c)
. Hence any factorization as above exactly corresponds to its colimit inclusion, whence the bijection. ∎

2.7
{\bf Spec}\dashv\Gamma
-adjunction for set-valued models and sheaf representation

2.7.1
.

Now recall that
\mathcal{S}
is terminal amongst Grothendieck topoi, and for any Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
, the terminal geometric morphism
!_{\mathcal{E}}:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
has for direct image part
\Gamma=\mathcal{E}(1,-)
. Now as
\mathbb{T}
is a finite limit theory, it is stable under direct image, so that
\Gamma
induces a functor

\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Gamma}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]

In particular for any locally
\mathbb{T}_{J}
-modelled topos
(\mathcal{E},E)
, we can apply
{!_{\mathcal{E}}}_{*}
to
wE
to get a set-valued
\mathbb{T}
-model
\Gamma E
, and for a morphism of locally
\mathbb{T}_{J}
-modelled topoi
(f,\phi):(\mathcal{E},E)\rightarrow(\mathcal{F},F)
, we have a morphism
\Gamma\phi^{\sharp}:\Gamma E\rightarrow\Gamma F
, as
\Gamma f_{*}F=\Gamma F
since direct images commute with global sections. Moreover, for a 2-cell
\alpha:(f,\phi)\Rightarrow(g,\psi)
, the equality
\psi^{\flat}F*\alpha^{\flat}=\phi^{\flat}
corresponds to an equality
\phi^{\sharp}=\alpha^{\sharp}*F\psi^{\sharp}
with
\alpha^{\sharp}:g_{*}\Rightarrow f_{*}
; but
\Gamma
sends
F*\alpha^{\sharp}
into an equality, so that
\alpha
is collapsed into the equality of the morphism
\Gamma\phi^{\sharp}=\Gamma\psi^{\sharp}
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
.
This defines a 2-functor

{\mathbb{T}_{J}^{{\bf Loc}}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Gamma}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}

Theorem 2.7.2
.

We have an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}\quad}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}_{J}^{{\bf Loc}}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

Let
(f,\phi):({\bf Spec}(B),\widetilde{B})\rightarrow(\mathcal{E},E)
be a morphism of locally modelled topoi, that is the data of a geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
and a local map
\phi^{\flat}:f^{*}\widetilde{B}\rightarrow E
. Then from
theorem

2.6.12
,
f
defines uniquely a local unit
f^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B}):\;!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B\rightarrow f^{*}\widetilde{B}
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
, which we can compose with the local map
\phi^{\flat}
to get a morphism into
E

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}\widetilde{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}}

which corresponds uniquely to a morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi^{\flat}f^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B}))^{\sharp}}

For the converse, any
f:B\rightarrow\Gamma E
with
(\mathcal{E},E)
a locally modelled topos defines uniquely a morphism
f^{\flat}:\;!_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}B\rightarrow E
in
\mathcal{E}
, whose etale-local factorization – given in
corollary

2.6.10
defines uniquely a morphism of modelled topoi

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(B),\widetilde{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{E},E)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x_{n_{f^{\flat}}},u_{f^{\flat}})}

where
x_{n_{f^{\flat}}}:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(B)
is the classifying morphism of the etale part
n_{\phi^{\flat}}\simeq x_{n_{f^{\flat}}}^{*}(\eta^{\flat}_{B})
and
u_{f^{\flat}}
is the local part of the factorization.
∎

This process can be explicitly generalized for
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
-objects in arbitrary Grothendieck topoi. But this will be better understood in the light of the concepts of
fibered sites
and
fibered topoi
. The next section is devoted to some prerequisites on this notion, but also contains a new variation of it and some results allowing to adapt it to our situation.

However before turning to the general case and our auxiliary results about fibered topoi, we should end this section with a
representability criterion
, explaining when the adjunction above has invertible units, that is, when a set valued model is representable as the global section object of its own structure sheaf.

2.7.3
.

Recall that a Grothendieck topology is
subcanonical
if representable presheaves are sheaves, equivalently if the sheafified Yoneda embedding still is fully faithful. This also amounts that, for any
J
-cover
(u_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
, the nerve diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\underset{i,j\in I}{\coprod}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c_{i}\times_{c}c_{j}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\underset{i\in I}{\coprod}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\text{\char 136\relax}_{u_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}}

exhibits
\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
as the coequalizer of the left parallel pair, not only after sheafification but already in in the presheaf category.

Recall we defined at
definition

1.2.2
the notion of extended
J
-covers as those
(n_{i}:B\rightarrow C_{i})_{i\in I}
obtained as pushouts of
J
-cover under an object
B
. In particular
J
-covers are extended
J
-covers. Now for an extended
J
-cover we can define its nerve

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}C_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{i,j\in I}C_{i}\times_{B}C_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle n_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}}

Theorem 2.7.4
.

Let
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)
be a geometry, with
{\bf Spec}\dashv\Gamma
the associated adjunction; then the following are equivalents:

-

any extended
J
-cover
(n_{i}:B\rightarrow C_{i})_{i\in I}
exhibits
B
as the limit of its nerve;

-

for any
B
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
the structural presheaf
{\rm cod}
is a sheaf for
J_{B}
;

-

for any
B
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
the unit
\eta^{\sharp}_{B}:B\rightarrow\Gamma\widetilde{B}
is invertible;

-

{\bf Spec}
is full and faithful.

Proof.

Observe that for any
J_{B}
cover

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}

the family
(m_{i}:{\rm cod}(n)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n_{i}))_{i\in I}
is an extended
J
-cover by the very definition of
J_{B}
. Hence if the first condition is satisfied, we have

{\rm cod}(n)\simeq\lim\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\rm cod}(n_{i})\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}{\rm cod}(n_{i}+_{n}n_{j})\big)

which exactly says that
{\rm cod}
is a sheaf for
J_{B}
.

As a consequence, the sheafification of the codomain functor provides with an isomorphism
{\rm cod}\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{J_{B}}{\rm cod}=\widetilde{B}
in
\mathbb{T}_{J}\big[[\mathcal{V}_{B},\mathcal{S}]\big]
, so that the structure sheaf coincides with the codomain functor. In particular we have

\Gamma\widetilde{B}={\rm cod}(1_{B})\simeq B

which exhibits the unit as an isomorphism. The last item is equivalent by generality on adjunctions.
∎

Definition 2.7.5
.

We say that
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)
has
sheaf representation
if it satisfies one, hence all of the equivalent conditions of
theorem

2.7.4
.

Proposition 2.7.6
.

If
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)
has sheaf representation, then the topology
J
is subcanonical.

Proof.

Take a
J
-cover
(\theta_{i}:\{\overline{x}_{i},\phi_{i}\}\rightarrow\{\overline{x},\phi\})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}
, with
(n_{\theta_{i}}:K_{\phi}\rightarrow K_{\phi_{i}})_{i\in I}
the corresponding family in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}
. Then for any
\{\overline{y},\psi\}
one has
\text{\char 136\relax}_{\{\overline{y},\psi\}}(\{\overline{x},\phi\})\simeq\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}](K_{\psi},K_{\phi})
. Then assuming that extended
J
-covers are limiting, we know that

K_{\phi}\simeq\lim\big(\prod_{i\in I}K_{\phi_{i}}\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}K_{\phi_{i}}+_{K_{\phi}}K_{\phi_{j}}\big)

Then, since the covariant representable preserve limits, we have

\text{\char 136\relax}_{\{\overline{y},\psi\}}(\{\overline{x},\phi\})\simeq\lim\big(\prod_{i\in I}\text{\char 136\relax}_{\{\overline{y},\psi\}}(\{\overline{x}_{i},\phi_{i}\})\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}\text{\char 136\relax}_{\{\overline{y},\psi\}}(\{\overline{x},\phi\})(\{\overline{x}_{i},\phi_{i}\}\times_{\{\overline{x},\phi\}}\{\overline{x}_{j},\phi_{j}\})\big)

This exactly says that the representable
\text{\char 136\relax}_{\{\overline{y},\psi\}}
is a sheaf for
J
, hence that
J
is subcanonical.
∎

2.7.7
.

Subcanonicity per se seems to be unsufficient in all generality to ensure sheaf representation. However, the situation is better when the topology consists of finite families – which, from the point of view of logic, means that
\mathbb{T}_{J}
is a special case of geometric extension of
\mathbb{T}
, namely a
coherent extension
. Actually all examples we will consider fall under this situation, which make this assumption fairly reasonable. This is part of
[
6
]
[Theorem 4.5.1], we give here a more detailed proof:

Proposition 2.7.8
.

Let
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)
be a geometry with
J
consisting of finite families. Then one has sheaf representation if and only if
J
is subcanonical.

Proof.

If
J
is subcanonical, then its cover corresponds to actual colimits in
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}
: that is, each cover
(m_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
in
J
exhibit
K=\lim_{i\in I}K_{i}
of the corresponding nerve diagram. From the first item of
theorem

2.7.4
, it suffices to prove that extended covers exhibits their domain as the limit of the corresponding nerve to ensure sheaf representation.

As
J
-covers are finite, so are extended
J
-covers; take some
B
and
(n_{i}:B\rightarrow B_{i})_{i\in I}
an extended
J
-cover. Define the category
J\downarrow(n_{i})_{i\in I}
whose objects are all triples
(K,a,(m_{i})_{i\in I})
consisting of a
J
-covers
(m_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})_{i\in I}
inducing
(n_{i})_{i\in I}
and morphisms
(m_{i})_{i\in I}\rightarrow(m^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
are morphisms between lifts exhibiting the codomain lift as a pushout :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i*}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{i*}a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i*}a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The functor
J\downarrow(n_{i})_{i\in I}
sending a lift
(K,a,(m_{i})_{i\in I})
to the underlying lift
a:K\rightarrow B
is clearly cofinal in the canonical cocone
\mathbb{T}_{[}\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}\downarrow B
. Similarly, for each
i
, the functor sending
(K,a,(m_{i})_{i\in I})
to the corresponding pushout projection
m_{i*}a:K_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}
is cofinal in
\mathbb{T}_{[}\mathcal{S}]_{\omega}\downarrow B_{i}
. Now commutativity of finite limits and filtered colimits ensures that

\underset{J\downarrow(n_{i})_{i\in I}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,K_{i}\simeq\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,\underset{J\downarrow(n_{i})_{i\in I}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,K_{i}

But on one hand one has

\displaystyle\underset{J\downarrow(n_{i})_{i\in I}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,K_{i}

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{J\downarrow(n_{i})_{i\in I}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,K\simeq B

by cofinality in the canonical cocone, and similarly on the other hand

\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,\underset{J\downarrow(n_{i})_{i\in I}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,K_{i}\simeq\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,B_{i}

This proves that extended
J
-covers exhibit their domain as the limit of their nerve.∎

3
Fibered sites and fibered topoi

Before describing the spectral site of a general modelled topos – generalizing the case of a mere set-valued model – we chose to devote some time to introduce a special enhancement of
[
24
]
[VI, part 7] notion of
fibered site
and
fibered topos
. Fibered sites are begotten when applying the Grothendieck construction to indexed categories returning sites as fibers; it is shown there that the topos of sheaves over a fibered site coincides with the category of cartesian sections of a corresponding direct fibration in Grothendieck topoi and direct image functors. In this source are considered fibered sites and topoi above base category without topology; we shall propose in this section an adaptation for the case of a topology on the base category, as it shall be used in the next section: we will recognize indeed the spectral site of a modelled topos as a fibered site over a definition site for the underlying topos. Though this section is not strictly required to understand the construction and can be skipped by the hurry reader, we think it is worth of interest as being close to the construction though which one externalize an internal site in a topos.

3.1
Fibered sites as oplax colimits

Definition 3.1.1
.

A
fibered lex site
on a small category
\mathcal{C}
is an indexed category
V:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
such that in each
c\in\mathcal{C}
,
V_{c}
is lex and equipped with a Grothendieck topology
J_{c}
such that for each
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
the corresponding transition functor
V_{s}:V_{c_{2}}\rightarrow V_{c_{1}}
is a morphism of lex site.

For a fibered site on small, lex category
\mathcal{C}
, one can consider the Grothendieck construction

\int V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{V}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{C}

at the indexed category
V
, which is the oplax colimit of
V
, equipped with the canonical oplax cocone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{c_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{c_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{c_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{c_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{s}}

Lemma 3.1.2
.

For a fibered lex site
V:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
over a lex category
\mathcal{C}
, the oplax colimit
\int V
is lex, as well as the fibration
p_{V}
.

Proof.

The finite limit of a finite diagram
F:I\rightarrow\int V
is constructed as follows: first take the limit
\lim_{i\in I}p_{V}(F(i))
in
\mathcal{C}
; then, we have a pseudococone diagram in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{p_{V}(F(i))}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,p_{V}(F(i))}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{p_{V}(F(j))}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{p_{V}(F(d))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{p_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{p_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

producing a finite diagram
(V_{p_{i}}(F(i)))_{i\in I}
in
V_{\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,p_{V}(F(i))}
, where one can take the limit
\lim_{i\in I}V_{p_{i}}(F(i))
. Then we have

\lim_{I}\,F\simeq(\lim_{i\in I}p_{V}(F(i)),\,\lim_{i\in I}V_{p_{i}}(F(i)))

∎

Remark 3.1.3
.

In the case of the oplax colimits we need the indexing category to have itself finite limits; when considering pseudocolimits, it will be sufficient to require it to be cofiltered, as we wont need the underlying cone to be limiting anymore thanks to localizations along cartesian morphisms.

3.1.4
.

Now we can equip
\int V
with a coarsest topology, the
fibered topology
, making the inclusions
\iota_{c}
lex continuous: define the topology

J_{V}=\langle\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\iota_{c}(J_{c})\rangle

as generated by the inclusion of all fiber topologies, then trivially each
\iota_{c}:(V_{c},J_{c})\hookrightarrow(\int V,J_{V})
is a morphism of lex sites; the covers in the
\iota_{c}(J_{c})
are called
horizontal families
.

3.2
Fibered topos and topos of sections

Now one can ask for a fibered site as seen above what is corresponding notion of sheaf topos. This is the purpose of the following notion

Definition 3.2.1
.

A
fibered topos
on a category
\mathcal{C}
is an indexed category
\mathcal{E}_{(-)}:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
such that for any
c\in\mathcal{C}
the fiber
\mathcal{E}_{c}
is a Grothendieck topos, and for each
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
the transition functor
\mathcal{E}_{s}:\mathcal{E}_{c_{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{c_{1}}
is the inverse image part of a geometric functor
f_{s}:\mathcal{E}_{c_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{c_{2}}
.

Then we can also consider the Grothendieck construction at a fibered topos and define the fibration

\int\mathcal{E}_{(-)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{\mathcal{E}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{C}

Remark 3.2.2
.

Observe that we consider here inverse image part, so that this Grothendieck construction is in particular an oplax colimit
in
{\bf Cat}
of the underlying categories of the fiber topoi.

3.2.3
.

Now, to a fibered site
V
, we can canonically associate a fibered topos whose fiber at
c
is the sheaf topos
{\bf Sh}(V_{c},J_{c})
and the transition at
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
is the inverse image of the geometric morphism
{\bf Sh}(V_{s}):{\bf Sh}(V_{c_{1}},J_{c_{1}})\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(V_{c_{2}},J_{c_{2}})
induced by
V_{s}
.
Then we can consider the Grothendieck construction associated to the fibered topos

\int{\bf Sh}(V_{(-)},J_{(-)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{p_{V}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{C}

Definition 3.2.4
.

For a Grothendieck fibration
p:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, we denote as
\Gamma(p)
the category whose objects are
sections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

and whose arrows are natural transformations between those sections. In particular,
cartesian sections
are sections
x
sending any arrow
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
to a cartesian morphism
x(s):x(c_{1})\rightarrow x(c_{2})
.

3.2.5
.

Moreover, observe that any fibered topos
\mathcal{E}_{(-)}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
defines also a Grothendieck fibration on
\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}
thanks to the adjunctions
f_{s}^{*}\dashv{f_{s}}_{*}
, where the fiber at
c
is still
\mathcal{E}_{c}
but the transition morphism at
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
is now the direct image functor
{f_{s}}_{*}:\mathcal{E}_{c_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{c_{2}}
. We denote as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle{\int}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{(-)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}}}

the associated fibration. In the following we call this fibration the
direct fibration
of the fibered topos
\mathcal{E}_{(-)}
.

Then in particular a section
X:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\int\mathcal{E}_{(-)}^{\prime}
of the direct fibration of a fibered topos returns at each object
c
an object
X_{c}
of the topos
\mathcal{E}_{c}
and at an arrow
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
a morphism
(s,X_{s}):(c_{2},X_{c_{2}})\rightarrow(c_{1},X_{c_{1}})
with
X_{s}:X_{c_{2}}\rightarrow{f_{s}}_{*}X_{c_{1}}
with
{f_{s}}_{*}
the direct image of the transition geometric morphism
\mathcal{E}_{s}
.

The following, which is
[
24
]
[Proposition 7.4.7], says that the sheaf topos over the indexed site is the category of sections of the direct fibration associated to the fibered topos constructed by sheafification of the fibers.

Proposition 3.2.6
.

Let
V:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
a fibered lex site over a small lex category; then we have an equivalence of categories

{\bf Sh}(\int V,J_{V})\simeq\Gamma(\overline{p_{V}}^{\prime})

3.2.7
.

Moreover, sheafification turns the oplax cone made of the inclusions
(\iota_{c})_{c\in\mathcal{C}}
into an oplax cocone of direct image part of geometric morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(V_{c_{1}},J_{c_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(\int V,J_{V})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(V_{c_{2}},J_{c_{2}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(i_{c_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(i_{c_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(V_{s})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\phi_{s}}_{*}}

where
{\phi_{s}}_{*}
is the mate of the transformation
\phi_{s}^{*}:{\bf Sh}(\iota_{c_{1}})^{*}{\bf Sh}(V_{s})^{*}\Rightarrow{\bf Sh}(\iota_{c_{2}})^{*}
induced from the cocone
\phi_{s}
. In the bicategory of Grothendieck topoi, where 2-cells direction is the one of their inverse image part, this becomes a lax cone. Then we also have the following:

Proposition 3.2.8
.

We have in the bicategory of Grothendieck topoi that

{\bf Sh}(\int V,J_{V})\simeq\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}}{{\textup{laxlim}}}\,{\bf Sh}(V_{c},J_{c})

Proof.

It would actually be expectable for oplaxcolimits of lex sites to be turned into oplaxlimits of topoi, as well as finite colimits of sites are turned into finite limits and filtered colimits into cofiltered limits. To see this, observe that a lax cone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(V_{c_{1}},J_{c_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(V_{c_{2}},J_{c_{2}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(V_{s})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{s}}}

is the same as an lax cocone of lex-continuous functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(V_{c_{1}},J_{c_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(V_{c_{2}},J_{c_{2}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c_{1}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c_{2}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{s}}^{*}}

which factorizes uniquely in
{\bf Cat}
through the oplax colimit
f^{*}:\int V\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, and this functor is both lex and
J_{V}
continuous since all its restrictions at fibers are lex continuous, so that it defines a geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(\int V,J_{V})
.
∎

3.2.9
.

From the construction above of the oplax limit, we can construct the pseudolimit in
{\bf Cat}
of a fibered topos. It can be shown that the cartesian morphisms in the oplax colimit
\int V
have a left calculus of fractions in the sense of Gabriel-Zisman, as explained in
[
24
]
[Proposition 6.4], so that we can consider the localization of the oplax colimit
\int V
at the cartesian morphisms; we know that this localization is the pseudocolimit of the pseudofunctor
V
in
{\bf Cat}
, that is,

{\textup{pscolim}}\;V\simeq\int V[\Sigma_{V}^{-1}]

where
\Sigma_{V}
denotes the class of cartesian morphisms. Moreover, the topology
J_{V}
is transferred to the localization – we still denote the induced topology as
J_{V}
. This provides the following expression of pseudolimits:

Proposition 3.2.10
.

If
V
is a fibered site on a small lex category
\mathcal{C}
, we have in the bicategory of Grothendieck topoi that

{\bf Sh}(\underset{\mathcal{C}}{{\textup{pscolim}}}\;V,J_{V})\simeq\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}}{{\textup{pslim}}}\;{\bf Sh}(V_{c},J_{c})

Moreover the latter is also equivalent to the category
\Gamma_{\textbf{Cart}}(p^{\prime}_{V})
of cartesian sections of the direct fibration.

Remark 3.2.11
.

In
[
24
]
[Theorem 8.2.3] the underlying category is just supposed to be cofiltered for the equivalence above to hold; in our case this condition is automatically satisfied as we supposed
\mathcal{C}
to be lex. Beware that the results above are not necessarily true for an arbitrary small category
\mathcal{C}
. In fact, it is not known whether the bicategory of Grothendieck topoi has arbitrary small pseudolimits.

As this is the content of
[
24
]
[Sections 6 and 8, in particular 8.2.3] (and has also its bilimit version for bifiltered diagrams at
[
12
]
[Theorem 2.4]) we do not prove it again.

3.3
Topos of continuous sections

We now focus rather on the following adaptation in the case where the underlying category is endowed with a Grothendieck topology, asking for a way to make the fibration in a fibered site a comorphism of sites.

3.3.1
.

First observe that any fibered lex site has a terminal section
1_{(-)}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\int V
associating to each
c
the terminal object
1_{V_{c}}
of
V_{c}
and to each
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
. In particular this is a cartesian section as the transitions morphisms
V_{s}
for each
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
are lex so that
V_{s}(1_{V_{c_{2}}})=1_{V_{c_{1}}}
, so the value of this section at
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
is the cartesian lift
\overline{s}:(c_{1},V_{s}(1_{V_{c_{2}}})\rightarrow(c_{2};1_{V_{c_{2}}})
. Now for a topology
J
on
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
a fibered site, observe that any covering family
(c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
can be lifted to a family
(\overline{s}_{i}:(c_{i},1_{V_{c_{i}}})\rightarrow(c,1_{V_{c}}))_{i\in I}
: we call such families
horizontal
. Then consider the topology

J_{V,J}=\langle J_{V}\cup 1_{V_{(-)}}(J)\rangle

where
1_{(-)}(J)
consists of all families of the form
(\overline{s}_{i}:(c_{i},1_{V_{c_{i}}})\rightarrow(c,1_{V_{c}}))_{i\in I}
.

Remark 3.3.2
.

In the context of geometries, we consider pretopologies on the fibers, and want actually to manipulate covers of a spectral pretopology rather than sieves of a topology: then it is worth precising that the result above can be rephrased in term of pretopology: if the fibers
V_{c}
are equipped with a Grothendieck pretopology
J^{\prime}_{c}
, and if one chooses a basis
J_{0}
for the topology
J
on the indexing category
\mathcal{C}
(for instance the maximal basis associated to
J
), then we can first generate a pretopology
J^{\prime}_{V,J}
on the oplaxcolimit from the image of those basis
\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\iota_{c}(J^{\prime}_{c})
and
1_{V_{(-)}}(J_{0})
, which are the vertical and horizontal covers. Then observe that

-

the pretopology jointly generated by the fiber basis is itself a basis for the topology on the oplax colimit, or even more directly:

J_{V}=\langle\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\iota_{c}(J^{\prime}_{c})\rangle

-

the pretopology jointly generated by the images
\iota_{c}(J^{\prime}_{c})
and
1_{V_{(-)}}(J_{0})
is a basis for
J_{V,J}
:

J_{V,J}=\langle J^{\prime}_{V,J}\rangle=\langle\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\iota_{c}(J^{\prime}_{c})\cup 1_{V_{(-)}}(J_{0})\rangle

We used the terminal element to canonically lift
J
-covers in
\mathcal{C}
to covers in
\int V
; but once the topology is generated from those data, we get actually horizontal covers by lifting
J
-covers at any object in a fiber. To see this, use the following general lemma expressing that cartesian lifts of an arrow form altogether a cartesian transformation:

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Let
V:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
be a lex fibration. Then for any
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
the following square is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{1},V_{s}(a_{1}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{2},a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{1},1_{V_{c_{1}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{2},1_{V_{c_{2}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,1_{V_{s}(a_{2})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{c_{2}},!_{V_{s}(a)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{c_{2}},!_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,1_{V_{s}(a_{1})})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Proof.

In any other square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{3},a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{2},a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{1},1_{V_{c_{1}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{2},1_{V_{c_{2}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,1_{V_{s}(a_{2})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u,!_{a^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{c_{2}},!_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t,f)}

the vertical component of the left map is forced to be the terminal map
!_{a^{\prime}}
as
V_{u}
preserves the terminal element. But then, such a square is the same as a situation testing the cartesianness of the lift
(s,1_{V_{s}(a)})
which always produces a unique map as the dashed arrow below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{1},V_{s}(a))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{2},a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{3},a_{3})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,1_{V_{s}(a)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t,f)}

which provides in particular the desired factorization.
∎

As a consequence, from we know that Grothendieck topologies are closed under pullback of covering families, it appears the following:

Corollary 3.3.4
.

Let
V
be a fibered site on a lex category
\mathcal{C}
and
J
a Grothendieck topology on
\mathcal{C}
. Then for any
J
-cover
(s_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
and any
a\in V_{c}
, the family
((s_{i},1_{V_{s_{i}}(a)}):(c_{i},V_{s_{i}}(a))\rightarrow(c,a))_{i\in I}
is a covering family in
J_{V,J}
.

As defined above, the topology
J_{V,J}
is the simplest that allows to induce a geometric morphism toward
{\bf Sh}(C,J)
. In fact:

Proposition 3.3.5
.

The topology
J_{V,J}
is the coarsest topology such that we have simultaneously the two following conditions:

-

for each
c
in
\mathcal{C}
, the inclusion
\iota_{c}:(V_{c_{1}},J_{c_{1}})\rightarrow(\int V,J_{V,J})
is a morphism of sites;

-

the fibration
p_{V}:(\int V,J_{V,J})\rightarrow(\mathcal{C},J)
is a comorphism of sites.

Moreover, as this topology refines the topology
J_{V}
, there is a corresponding inclusion of topos

{\bf Sh}(\int V,J_{V,J})\hookrightarrow{\bf Sh}(\int V,J_{V})

exhibiting objects of
{\bf Sh}(\int V,J_{V,J})
as a specific kind of sections, because the right topos was the topos of sections. The intuition is that some notion of continuity relative to the base topology is involved. We introduce the following notion to this end:

Definition 3.3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{(-)}
be a fibered topos on a lex site
(\mathcal{C},J)
. Then by a
continuous section of

\mathcal{E}_{(-)}
we mean a section of the associated direct fibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int\mathcal{E}_{(-)}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}

such that for any
J
-covering family
(s_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
the lifting
(X_{s_{i}}:X_{c}\rightarrow{f_{s_{i}}}_{*}X_{c_{i}})_{i\in I}
exhibits
X_{c}
a limit in the fiber
\mathcal{E}_{c}
of the diagram

X_{c}=\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,\Big(\prod_{i\in I}{f_{s_{i}}}_{*}X_{c_{i}}\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}{f_{s_{i}}}_{*}{f_{s^{i}_{ij}}}_{*}X_{c_{ij}}\Big)
