where the double arrow is induced from the transitions
({f_{s_{i}}}_{*}(X_{s_{ij}^{i}}):{f_{s_{i}}}_{*}X_{c_{i}}\rightarrow{f_{s_{i}}}_{*}{f_{s^{i}_{ij}}}_{*}X_{c_{ij}})_{i,j\in I}
over the nerve of the cover
(s_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
.
We denote as
\Gamma_{J}(\overline{p_{E}}^{\prime})
the category of continuous sections and natural transformations between them.

Theorem 3.3.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},J)
be a small lex site,
V:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
a fibered lex site on
\mathcal{C}
with
\overline{p_{V}}:\int{\bf Sh}(V_{(-)},J_{(-)})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
the associated fibered topos and
\overline{p_{V}}^{\prime}:\int{\bf Sh}(V_{(-)},J_{(-)})^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}
the corresponding direct fibration. Then one has an equivalence of categories

{\bf Sh}(\int V,J_{V,J})=\Gamma_{J}(\overline{p_{V}}^{\prime})

Proof.

For one direction, observe that any sheaf
X
in
{\bf Sh}(\int V,J_{V,J})
can be composed with the duals of the fiber inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{c_{1}}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int V^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{c_{2}}^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{c_{1}}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{c_{2}}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{s}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{s}^{{\rm op}}}

and as the topology of each fiber is part of the topology
J_{V,J}
the restriction
X\iota_{c}^{{\rm op}}
is a sheaf for the topology
J_{c}
, hence is an object of the sheaf topos
{\bf Sh}(V_{c},J_{c})
; moreover, for each
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
, the diagram below provides us by whiskering with a transformation

X*\phi_{s}^{{\rm op}}:X\iota_{c_{2}}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow X\iota_{c_{1}}^{{\rm op}}V_{s}^{{\rm op}}

But precomposition with
V_{s}
is what the direct image
{\bf Sh}(V_{s})_{*}
consists in. So we can associate to
X
the section
\widehat{X}:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\int\mathcal{E}
with
\widehat{X}_{c}=X\iota^{{\rm op}}_{c}={\bf Sh}(\iota_{c})_{*}X
and
\widehat{X}_{s}=X*\phi_{s}
. Now we have to check that
X
is a continuous section. But we know that
X
is a sheaf for the horizontal topology, and also relative to any horizontal cover
((s_{i},1_{V_{s_{i}}(a)}):(c_{i},V_{s_{i}}(a))\rightarrow(c,a))_{i\in I}
, which hence is sent on a limit diagram

\widehat{X}_{c}(a)=\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,\Big(\prod_{i\in I}\widehat{X}_{c_{i}}(V_{s_{i}}(a))\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}\widehat{X}_{c_{ij}}(V_{s_{ij}^{i}}V_{s_{i}}(a))\Big)

where

V_{s_{ij}^{i}}V_{s_{i}}(a)=V_{s_{ij}^{j}}V_{s_{j}}(a)

for each
i,j\in I
: but from the fact that limits are pointwise in categories of sheaves, this is sufficient to ensure that
\widehat{X}
is a continuous section.

For the converse, suppose we have a continuous section
\widehat{X}:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\int\mathcal{E}_{(-)}^{\prime}
. Then each
X_{c}
is a sheaf on
(V_{c},J_{c})
; by the property of the oplaxcolimit, the data of the cocone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{c_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{c_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{c_{1}}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{c_{2}}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{{\rm op}}_{s}}

induces a unique functor
X:\int V^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
whose restriction at each fiber coincides with
X_{c}
and whose whiskering with some
\phi_{s}^{{\rm op}}
is
X_{s}
. Hence
X
is a sheaf for the topology
\langle\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}}J_{c}\rangle
, with values
X(c,a)=X_{c}(a)
for
a\in V_{c}
, and
X(s,u)
is obtained as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{c_{2}}(a_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{c_{1}}(V_{s}(a_{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{c_{1}}(a_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(s,u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{s})_{a_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{c_{1}}(u)}

where
(X_{s})_{a_{2}}
is the component at
a_{2}
of the natural transformation
X_{c_{2}}\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(V_{s})_{*}X_{c_{1}}
and
X_{c_{1}}(u)
is the restriction map of
X_{c_{1}}
at
u:a_{1}\rightarrow V_{s}(a_{2})
. But now, being continuous as a section, for any
J
-cover
(s_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
the transitions
(X_{s_{i}}:X_{c}\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(V_{s})_{*}X_{c_{i}})_{i\in I}
form a limit diagram in
{\bf Sh}(V_{c},J_{c})
, and as limits are pointwise in categories of sheaves, this means in particular that in
1_{V_{c}}
, the induced horizontal cover
((s_{i},\overline{s_{i}}):(c_{i},1_{V_{c_{i}}})\rightarrow(c,1_{V_{c}})
where
1_{V_{c_{i}}}=V_{s_{i}}(1_{V_{c}}))_{i\in I}
is sent to a limit diagram

X(c,1_{V_{c}})=\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,\Big(\prod_{i\in I}X(c_{i},1_{V_{c_{i}}})\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}X(c_{ij},1_{V_{c_{ij}}})\Big)

which exactly means that
X
is a sheaf for the topology
1_{V_{(-)}}(J)
.

Now it is clear that those two processes are mutually inverse from the fact that
\int V
is the oplaxcolimit,
X
and
\widehat{X}
are mutually determined as functors.
∎

4
Spectral site of a modelled topos

In this section we describe the spectral site of an arbitrary
\mathbb{T}
-modelled topos for a fixed geometry
(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{V},J)
. While this definition of the spectral site was given in
[
6
]
, we think it is worth giving new details about the geometric and fibrational aspects involved in this construction. As we shall see, the spectral site of a sheaf of
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
-models will be constructed as a fibered site made of the spectral site of its values at basic opens of the topos it lives in. Then from the results of the previous section, the spectrum will be exhibited as a topos of global sections of the direct fibration of the fibered topos constituted of the spectra of the local values. We shall also describe the structure sheaf in this context.

Previously we proved that, in the case of set-valued models, the functor returning the topos of sheaves over the spectral site together with its structure sheaf was left adjoint to a certain global section functor. In this section, we shall see that the 2-functor associating to an arbitrary modelled topos the topos constructed from the spectral site together with its structure sheaf also defines a left adjoint, this time to the inclusion of locally modelled topoi. Equivalently, we prove that the spectral topos classifies local units modulo inverse images.

4.1
The spectral site as a fibered site

4.1.1
.

For a Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{F}
with a lex site of presentation
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}},J_{\mathcal{F}})
and
F
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{F}]
, each local value
F(c)
at basic open
c
of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
is a set valued
\mathbb{T}
-model, while for a morphism
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
we have a morphism of
\mathbb{T}
-models
F(s):F(c_{2})\rightarrow F(c_{1})
. This defines a pseudofunctor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{V}_{F(-)}^{{\rm op}}}}{{\rightarrow}}&{\bf Lex}\\
c&\mapsto&\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}^{{\rm op}}\\
c_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}c_{2}&\mapsto&\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{2})}^{{\rm op}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(s_{*})^{{\rm op}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{1})}^{{\rm op}}\end{array}

At an arrow
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
, one has a pushout functor

\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{2})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F(s)_{*}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{1})}

relating the fiber inclusions through a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(c_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(c_{1})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{c_{2}}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{c_{1}}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)_{*}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{s}}

whose component
\phi^{s}_{n}
at an object
n:F(c_{2})\rightarrow C
is given by the opposite of the pushout map
(n_{*}F(s))^{{\rm op}}
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(c_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(c_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)_{*}C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)_{*}n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{*}F(s)}

The data of the fibers inclusions
\iota_{c}:\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(c)}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F}
together with those transformations define an oplax cocone exhibiting
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F}
as the oplax colimit of the indexed category
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(-)}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
. Beware that the cocone made of the etale generator themselves
\mathcal{V}_{F(-)}:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
is a
lax cocone
.

Remark 4.1.2
.

Beware here that the indexed site has as value
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}^{{\rm op}}
and not
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
itself. The applications of the previous section will hence require carefull substitution of
V
with
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}
…

Definition 4.1.3
.

Define the category
\mathcal{V}_{F}
as the following lax colimit in
{\bf Lex}

\mathcal{V}_{F}=\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{laxcolim}}}\;\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}

Remark 4.1.4
.

Observe we have then an oplax colimit

\mathcal{V}_{F}^{{\rm op}}=\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{oplaxcolim}}}\;\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}^{{\rm op}}

which will be the underlying category of the spectral site.

More explicitely,
\mathcal{V}_{F}
has as objects the pairs

(c,n)
with
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
n
a morphism in
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
, that is some
n:F(c)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
gotten as a pushout of map in
\mathcal{V}
. As morphisms, it has the pairs
(s,h):(c_{1},n_{1})\rightarrow(c_{2},n_{2})
with
s:c_{2}\in c_{1}
and
f:{\rm cod}(n_{1})\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n_{2})
related by a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Now recall that each
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
is part of the spectral site
(\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}^{{\rm op}},J_{F(c)})
of
F(c)
. Moreover we have the following:

Proposition 4.1.5
.

For each
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
we have a morphism of lex sites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{2})}^{{\rm op}},J_{F(c_{2})})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{1})}^{{\rm op}},J_{F(c_{1})})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)_{*}}

Proof.

For a family in
\mathcal{V}_{F(c^{\prime})}
obtained as a pushout along some
a
of a covering family
(n_{i}:K\rightarrow K_{i})
of finite presentation under
{\rm cod}(n)
, then composition of pushouts ensures that this family is transferred into a covering family of
F(s)_{*}n
in
\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{2})}
as visualised below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(s)_{*}{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(s)_{*}{\rm cod}(l_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)_{*}n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i*}F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{*}F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)_{*}n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

∎

4.1.6
.

From
lemma

3.1.2
we know that the oplax colimit
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(-)}
inherits finite limits of the fibers
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}^{{\rm op}}
and the basis
\mathcal{C}
: for a finite diagram
((c_{i},n_{i}))_{i\in I}
one first computes the finite limit
(p_{i}:\lim_{i\in I}c_{i}\rightarrow c_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
, which is sent to a cocone
((F(p_{i}):F(c_{i})\rightarrow F(\lim_{i\in I}c_{i}))_{i\in I}
. Then pushing the
n_{i}
along the transition functor
F(p_{i})
defines a finite diagram
(F(p_{i})_{*}n_{i})_{i\in I}
in
F(\lim_{i\in I})
, and since
\mathcal{V}_{F(\lim_{i\in I})}
has finite colimits, we have

\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,(c_{i},n_{i})\simeq(\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,c_{i},\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{colim}}}\,F(p_{i})_{*}n_{i})

In particular, pullbacks are computed as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{1}\times_{c}c_{2},F(p_{1})_{*}F(u_{1})_{*}n_{1}+_{F(p)_{*}n}F(p_{2})_{*}F(u_{2})_{*}n_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{2},n_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{1},n_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u_{2},f_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u_{1},f_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where
F(p_{1})_{*}F(u_{1})_{*}n_{1}+_{F(p)_{*}n}F(p_{2})_{*}F(u_{2})_{*}n_{2})
is the pushout in
\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{1}\times_{c}c_{2})}
of the span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(p)_{*}n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(p_{2})_{*}F(u_{2})_{*}n_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(p_{1})_{*}F(u_{1})_{*}n_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p)_{*}\langle n_{1},f_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p)_{*}\langle n_{2},f_{2}\rangle}

4.2
The spectrum from the fibered spectral site

4.2.1
.

Observe that the fibration
p_{F}:\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
has a left adjoint

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F}^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F}}

sending an object
c
to the pair
(c,1_{F(c)})
with the identity arrow
1_{F(c)}:F(c)\rightarrow F(c)
, which is etale and finitely presented (and is the terminal object of
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(c)}
).

4.2.2
.

Applying what was done in the previous section, if we fix
J_{\mathcal{F}}
as a subcanonical pretopology such that
\mathcal{F}\simeq{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}},J_{\mathcal{F}})
, we can equip the oplax colimit
\mathcal{V}_{F}
with the pretopology induced jointly from the spectral pretopologies of the local values and the underlying pretopology, that is,

J_{F}=\langle\iota_{{F}}(J_{\mathcal{F}})\cup\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}\iota_{c}(J_{F(c)})\rangle

In other words this pretopology is the pretopology jointly generated from
horizontal
families of the form

((c,1_{c})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(s_{i},F(s_{i}))}}{{\longrightarrow}}(c_{i},1_{c_{i}}))_{i\in I}\;\textrm{ with }(c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i}}}{{\rightarrow}}c_{i})_{i\in I}\in J^{{\rm op}}_{\mathcal{F}}(c)

and
vertical families
of the form

((c,n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1_{c},m_{i})}}{{\longrightarrow}}(c,n_{i}))_{i\in I}\textrm{ with }\Bigg(\hbox to110.71pt{\vbox to43.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 55.3562pt\lower-21.81943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Remark 4.2.3
.

In general, it can be quite difficult to give an explicit description of a topology generated this way; however the pretopology generated is more easy to handle. In this context we can make the following remarks. Recall that in a fibered category, any map factorizes uniquely as a vertical arrow followed by an horizontal, cartesian arrow: here this corresponds to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d,m)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d,F(s)_{*}n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{d},\langle f,m\rangle)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,1_{F(s)_{*}n})}

where
\langle f,m\rangle
is the map induced from the pushout property.

By the pullback axiom of pretopology, observe that if
(s_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
is covering in
J_{\mathcal{F}}
and
n
is in
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
then the family of cartesian lifts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{i},F(s_{i})_{*}n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s_{i},1_{F(s_{i})_{*}n})}

is a cover for
J_{F}
as being the pullback of the family
\iota_{F}((s_{i})_{i\in I}
along the morphism
(c,n)\rightarrow(c,1_{F(c)})
by
lemma

3.3.3
.

For
s:d\rightarrow c
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
n
in
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
, then the identity arrow
1_{F(s)_{*}n}
is a
J_{F(d)}
cover of
F(s)_{*}n
as it is induced by pushing out the identity map of
K
for any
a:K\rightarrow F(c)
. Then a cartesian morphism is a covering singleton as soon as
s
itself is a covering singleton in
J_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Now, for any
(s_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
in
J_{\mathcal{F}}
and
(m_{j}:n\rightarrow n_{j})_{j\in I^{\prime}}
a cover in
J_{F(c)}
, then for each
i\in I
the pushout family
(F(s_{i})_{*}m_{j}:F(s_{i})_{*}n\rightarrow F(s_{i})_{*}n_{j})_{j\in I^{\prime}}
is a cover in
J_{F(c_{i})}
.

But now, by transitivity axiom, we have a particular kind of covering families in
J_{F}
constructed as follows. Take a covering family
(s_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
in
J_{\mathcal{F}}(c)
, an object
n
in
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
, and for each
i\in I
a covering family
(m_{ij}:F(s_{i})_{*}n\rightarrow n_{ij})_{i\in I_{i}}
in
J_{F(c_{i})}
: then the induced family

(\hbox to140.7pt{\vbox to20.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 70.34921pt\lower-8.84027pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-70.34921pt}{-2.31946pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -25.76 0.25 L 39.55 0.25}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{28.78606pt}{0.18054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-21.94pt}{4.50551pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})_{i\in I,j\in I_{i}}

must be covering in
J_{F}
.

However beware that it may happen that there will be families
(s_{i},d_{i})_{i\in I}
in
J_{F}
whose underlying family
(s_{i})_{i\in I}
is not in
J_{\mathcal{F}}
. Otherwise the projection
p_{F}
would be a morphism of site, which is not true in general.

Definition 4.2.4
.

Define the
spectral site
of the
\mathbb{T}
-modelled topos
(\mathcal{F},F)
as the site
(\mathcal{V}_{F}^{{\rm op}},J_{F})
. Then define the
(Coste) spectrum
as

{\bf Spec}(\mathcal{F},F)={\bf Sh}(\mathcal{V}_{F}^{{\rm op}},J_{F})

In particular, this construction generalizes the construction of the spectrum of a set valued
\mathbb{T}
-model, as those ones just are the modelled topos of the form
(*,B)
with
B
seen as the constant sheaf of
\mathbb{T}
-models on the point.

4.2.5
.

It is worth understanding how the spectrum
{\bf Spec}(F)
and its structure sheaf
\widetilde{F}
compare to the spectra
{\bf Spec}(F(c))
and structure sheaves
\widetilde{F(c)}
of the local values. Recall that the oplax colimit
\mathcal{V}_{F}^{{\rm op}}\simeq{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}^{{\rm op}}
is canonically equipped with an induced pretopology

J_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(-)}}=\langle\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}\iota_{c}(J_{F(c)})\rangle

such that we have a geometric equivalence in the bicategory of Grothendieck topoi

{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{V}_{F},J_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(-)}})\simeq\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}{{\textup{laxlim}}}\,{\bf Spec}(F(c))

Now from the topology
J_{F}
is generated from
J_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(-)}}
together with
\iota_{F}(J_{\mathcal{F}})
we have a geometric inclusion

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}{{\textup{laxlim}}}\,{\bf Spec}(F(c))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{i}_{F}}

whose inverse image part is the sheafification functor
\mathfrak{a}_{\iota_{F}(\mathcal{F})}
: intuitively, this functor “corrects” sheaves for the generated pretopology
J_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(-)}}
into sheaves for the spectral pretopology
J_{F}
.

Precomposing
\mathfrak{i}_{F}
together with the limiting projections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}{{\textup{laxlim}}}\,{\bf Spec}(F(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F(c))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{c}}

produces a lax cone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({{\bf Spec}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F(c))})_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{c}\mathfrak{i}_{F}}

where
p_{c}\mathfrak{i}_{F}={\bf Sh}(\iota_{c})
. But now recall that
{\bf Sh}(F(s)_{*})={\bf Spec}(F(s))
, seeing
F(s):F(c_{2})\rightarrow F(c_{1})
as a morphism in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
: hence the transition morphisms of this cone are given as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F(c_{1}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F(c_{2}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\iota_{c_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(F(s))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\iota_{c_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{s}}

4.3
The spectrum as a topos of continuous sections and the canonical fibration

4.3.1
.

Now we turn to the relation between the spectrum and its base topos. We have a fibered lex site
\mathcal{V}_{F(-)}^{{\rm op}}:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Lex}
.
Now recall that each of the sites
(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(c)},J_{F(c)})
is the spectral site of
F(c)
, that is,
{\bf Spec}(F(c))={\bf Sh}(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(c)},J_{F(c)})
. Then the associated fibered topos has the spectrum of the
F(c)

as fiber, that is, we have a fibered topos

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\int{\bf Spec}(F(-))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p_{F}}}

whose transition morphisms are the inverse image
{\bf Spec}(F(s))^{*}:{\bf Spec}(F(c_{2}))\rightarrow{\bf Spec}(F(c_{1}))
for
s:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
. We can consider its associated direct fibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\int{\bf Spec}(F(-))^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p_{F}}^{\prime}}

Recognize now in the definition of the topology
J_{F}
that it is generated from the horizontal and vertical families as done in the previous section. Applying
theorem

3.3.7
we have the following:

Theorem 4.3.2
.

The spectrum of
F
is the topos of continuous sections of the direct fibration associated to the fibered spectral topos:

{\bf Spec}(F)\simeq\Gamma_{J}(\overline{p_{F}}^{\prime})

4.3.3
.

We saw above that the fibration of site
p_{F}
is equipped here with a left adjoint
\iota_{F}
; moreover from the way
J_{F}
was defined, we know trivially
\iota_{F}
to define a morphism of site. Conversely the projection
p_{F}
, though not being a morphism of site, is a comorphism of site. Hence we are in a situation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F},J_{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}},J_{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F}}

with a comorphism of sites right adjoint to a morphism of sites. Hence from
[
3
]
[Proposition 3.14] or also
[
19
]
[Theorem VII.10.5] they both induce a same
geometric morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{F}}

which moreover lifts to an adjoint functor between categories of
\mathbb{T}
-models

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(F)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{F}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{F*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

with the inverse and direct part respectively given by

h_{F}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}((-)\circ{p_{F}^{{\rm op}}})={\textup{lan}}_{\iota_{F}^{{\rm op}}}(-)

h_{F*}=\mathfrak{a}_{J_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}(p_{F}^{{\rm op}},-))=(-)\circ\iota_{F}^{{\rm op}}

Beware that in the computation of the inverse image
h_{F}^{*}
, precomposing a
J_{\mathcal{F}}
-sheaf with the comorphism
p_{F}^{{\rm op}}
does not return a
J_{F}
sheaf (since the vertical families have no reason to be taken into account by this process), hence the necessity of the further sheafification. Observe that we must op the functors
\iota_{F}
and
p_{F}
as the involved sheaves of
\mathbb{T}
-objects have
\mathcal{V}_{F}
and
\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}
as domains.

4.4
The structure sheaf

Again, the structure sheaf is obtained as the sheafification of the codomain functor: observe this latter is the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}_{F}}

defined as acting fiberwisely as the codomain functor, that is, sending
(c,n)
on
{\rm cod}_{c}(n)
, seeing
n
as an object of
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
, and
(s,f):(c_{1},n_{1})\rightarrow(c_{2},n_{2})
as the underlying map
f:{\rm cod}(n_{1})\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n_{2})
. In other word
{\rm cod}_{F}
is the functor induced by the laxcolimit property of
\mathcal{V}_{F}
from the laxcocone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{1})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}_{c_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}_{c_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\phi_{s}}}

Definition 4.4.1
.

The
structure sheaf
of the modelled topos
(\mathcal{F},F)
is the sheaf

\widetilde{F}=\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}{\rm cod}_{F}

obtained as the sheafification of the presheaf of
\mathbb{T}
-objects defined on
\mathcal{V}_{F}
as the codomain functor

\overline{F}:(c,n)\mapsto{\rm cod}(n)

where we denote as
\gamma:{\rm cod}\rightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}{\rm cod}
the unit of the sheafification at
{\rm cod}
.

Remark 4.4.2
.

Hence this structure sheaf is obtained as applying successively the sheafification of vertical, then horizontal families.

4.4.3
.

Now at the level of the structure sheaves, observe that the data of all the
(\widetilde{F(c)})_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}
induce a lax cocone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}])_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F(c)}}

whose transition 2-cells are given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{1})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F(c_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F(c_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi_{s}}}

where
\overline{\phi_{s}}
is the morphism of sheaves induced from the comparison functor obtained from the pushout maps
\phi_{s}=(n_{*}F(s))_{n\in\mathcal{V}_{F(c_{2})}}
after sheafification

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}_{c_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{s*}{\rm cod}_{c_{1}}\simeq{\rm cod}_{c_{1}}F_{s*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F(c_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{s})_{*}\widetilde{F(c_{2})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{c_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{c_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi_{s}}}

Then by the universal property of the oplax colimit in
{\bf Cat}
this induces uniquely a canonical functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Or in other terms, we know that

{p_{c}}_{*}\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}\simeq\widetilde{F(c)}

Moreover, for each
\widetilde{F}(c)
is a
J_{F(c)}
sheaf of
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
-objects, the induced
\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a
J_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(-)}}
-sheaf.

Recalling that the lax limit topos is also the category of all sections of the direct fibration
\Gamma(\overline{p_{F}}^{\prime})
, this object can also be described as the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}{{\textup{laxlim}}}\,{\bf Spec}(F(c))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}}

sending each
\{\overline{x},\phi\}
to the section of the direct fibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle{\int}{\bf Spec}(F(-))^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F(-)}(\{\overline{x},\phi\})}

sending
c
to
\widetilde{F(c)}(\{\overline{x},\phi\})
in
{\bf Spec}(F(c))
.

Beware however that, at this step, this induced sheaf is not yet a sheaf for
J_{F}
as the
\iota_{F}(J_{\mathcal{F}})
-families are not considered in
J_{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F(-)}}
.

Lemma 4.4.4
.

The structure sheaf is the sheafification for the horizontal topology of the induced presheaf

\widetilde{F}\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{\iota_{\mathcal{F}}}(\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}})

Proof.

Each
\widetilde{F(c)}
is the sheafification of the codomain functor for the spectral topology at
c
; but the topology in the lax limit topos is exactly the topology jointly generated by the fiberwise spectral topology, while the transition functors are continuous: hence being a sheaf for this topology amounts to being locally a sheaf for the fiberwise topologies, and we have

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F(c)}}{\rm cod}_{c}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}{\bigcup}\iota_{c}(J_{F(c)})}\langle{\rm cod}_{c}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}

but then it suffice to apply the sheafification for the horizontal topology to get the structure sheaf.
∎

4.4.5
.

In particular, there is a canonical way to compare the global structure sheaf to the structure sheaves associated to local values. At any
c
the codomain functor over
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
and the restriction along
\iota_{c}
of the codomain functor over
\mathcal{V}_{F}
coincide. Yet however, this is not sufficient for the structure sheaves to coincide after restriction. As a direct image,
{\bf Sh}(\iota_{c})_{*}
does not commute with sheafification: hence
{\bf Sh}(\iota_{c})_{*}\widetilde{F}={\bf Sh}(\iota_{c})_{*}\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}{\rm cod}
needs not be the sheafification of
{\rm cod}
for
J_{F(c)}
, though it is a sheaf for
J_{F(c)}
because
\iota_{c}
is a morphism of site. Hence by the universal property of the sheafification, we have a factorization in
\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(F(c))]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\iota_{c}}_{*}{\rm cod}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{i}_{F(c)}\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F(c)}}{\rm cod}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\iota_{c}}_{*}\mathfrak{i}_{F}\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}{\rm cod}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{{\rm cod}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\iota_{c}}_{*}(\gamma_{{\rm cod}})}

where
{\iota_{c}}_{*}\mathfrak{i}_{F}=\mathfrak{i}_{F}{\bf Sh}(\iota_{c})_{*}
and
\mathfrak{i}_{F(c)}
is the geometric inclusion
{\bf Spec}(F(c))\hookrightarrow[\mathcal{V}_{F(c)},\mathcal{S}]
. We denote this morphism as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F(c)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(\iota_{c})_{*}\widetilde{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{c}^{\sharp}}

and its mate through the
{\bf Sh}(\iota_{c})
adjunction as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sh}(\iota_{c})^{*}\widetilde{F(c)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{c}^{\flat}}

Theorem 4.4.6
.

The structure sheaf
\widetilde{F}
is a local object in
\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(F)]
.

Proof.

In fact we are going to prove that the structure sheaf is already a local object in the lax limit topos even before sheafification, that is, we prove that
\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is already a local object in
{\textup{laxlim}}_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}{\bf Spec}(F(c))
,
We claim this functor to be a local object. For each
(n_{i}:K_{\phi}\rightarrow K_{\phi_{i}})_{i\in I}
in
J
, we must display an epimorphism in the category of sections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{\displaystyle{\coprod}}\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\{\overline{x}_{i},\phi_{i}\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\{\overline{x},\phi\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\theta_{n_{i}})\rangle_{i\in I}}

As in the set-valued case, this is obtained by proving that this morphism comes from a local epimorphism in
\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F}}
given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{\displaystyle{\coprod}}\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}][(K_{\phi_{i}},{\rm cod}_{F}])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}][(K_{\phi},{\rm cod}_{F}])}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}][(n_{i},{\rm cod}_{F}])\rangle_{i\in I}}

For each
(c,n)
, we know that the restriction
\langle\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}][(n_{i},{\rm cod}_{c}])\rangle_{i\in I}
at
c
is a local epimorphism, as seen in
proposition

2.5.3
since, for each
a:K_{\phi}\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
, the cover induced as the pushout
(a_{*}n_{i})_{i\in I}
produced a family of antecedents of
a
ensuring local surjectivity: but then the family
((c,n)\rightarrow(c,a_{*}n_{i}n))_{i\in I}
is itself convenient in
\widehat{V^{{\rm op}}_{F}}
, and being a family of for a vertical cover, this proves
\langle\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}][(n_{i},{\rm cod}_{c}])\rangle_{i\in I}
to be a local epimorphism relative to the vertical topology
\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}{\bigcup}\iota_{c}(J_{F(c)})
.

Since
\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is the sheafification of
{\rm cod}_{F}
for the vertical topology, this proves the induced map
\langle\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\theta_{n_{i}})\rangle_{i\in I}
to be an epimorphism in the lax limit topos. But now, from
lemma

4.4.4
, for the structure sheaf is the sheafification of
\langle\widetilde{F(c)}\rangle_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}
along the horizontal topology, and sheafification preserves epimorphisms, we deduce
\widetilde{F}
to be a local object in
{\bf Spec}(F)
∎

4.4.7
.

Now we turn to the canonical morphisms of sheaves associated with the structure sheaf. At an object
(c,n)
of the spectral site
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F}
we are provided with a canonical arrow given by the composite of
n
with the sheafification

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}{\rm cod})(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{(c,n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{F})_{(c,n)}}

This map can be shown to be natural in
(c,n)
, and having in mind that
F(c)=F(p_{F}^{{\rm op}}(c,n))
, this defines a natural transformation in the category of presheaves
[\mathcal{V}_{F},\mathcal{S}]
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ p_{F}^{{\rm op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{i}_{F*}(w\widetilde{F})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{F}}

(where we also denote
\mathfrak{i}_{F}:{\bf Spec}(F)\hookrightarrow[\mathcal{V}_{F},\mathcal{S}]
), which in turns factorizes uniquely through the sheafification in
{\bf Spec}(F)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h_{F}^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F}^{\flat}}

Moreover, its mate

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h_{F*}w\widetilde{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F}^{\sharp}}

along the adjunction
h^{*}_{F}\dashv h_{F*}
indexes the values of
\eta^{\flat}_{F}
at the top element of the fibers, as we have
h_{F*}w\widetilde{F}(c)=w\widetilde{F}(\iota_{F}(c))=w\widetilde{F}(c,1_{F(c)})
, so that we have

(\eta^{\sharp}_{F})_{c}=(\eta^{\flat}_{F})_{(c,1_{F(c)})}

Proposition 4.4.8
.

The natural transformation
\eta_{F}^{\flat}
is an etale map in
\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(F)]
, while
\eta_{F}^{\sharp}
is etale in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{F}]
.

Proof.

By naturality of the unit of the sheafification
\gamma:{\rm cod}\Rightarrow\widetilde{F}
, the triangles above are natural in
(c,n)
and exhibit a factorization of
\eta_{F}
in the category of
\mathbb{T}
-models in the presheaf topos
[\mathcal{V}_{F},\mathcal{S}]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ p_{F}^{{\rm op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{i}_{F*}w\widetilde{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{{\rm cod}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{F}}

where
\nu_{F}
is the canonical map induced from all the
n:F(c)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
for
(c,n)
in
\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F}
, which is etale as it is pointwise etale. But now, as
{\mathfrak{i}_{F}}_{*}
is full and faithful with left adjoint
\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}
, we have a natural isomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}\mathfrak{i}_{F*}\simeq 1
, so that the aforementioned triangle is sent after sheafification to the following triangle in
\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(F)]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h_{F}^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w\widetilde{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}{\rm cod}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}\nu_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}\gamma_{{\rm cod}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F}^{\flat}}

where
{\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}\gamma_{{\rm cod}}}
is an isomorphism, while
\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F}}\nu_{F}
still is etale as etale maps are stable under inverse images. Hence, factorizing it as an etale map followed by an isomorphism, we have proven
\eta_{F}^{\flat}
to be an etale arrow in
\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(F)]
. From the equality
(\eta^{\sharp}_{F})_{c}=(\eta^{\flat}_{F})_{(c,1_{F(c)})}
, we have that
\eta^{\sharp}_{F}
is pointwise etale, hence is etale in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{F}]
.
∎

4.4.9
.

These data define a morphism of modelled topoi

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F},F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(F),w\widetilde{F})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{F},\eta_{F}^{\flat})}

5
Functoriality of
{\bf Spec}

Let us examine functoriality of
{\bf Spec}
. As we shall see, this is far from a trivial fact and is obtained through several steps; those complications were left totally implicit in the other sources on the topic, as they gave no precision on the functoriality: we think it justifies to devote some effort to clarify this aspect.

We saw in
1.4.3
that
\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}
admitted two factorizations systems (Vertical, Cartesian) and (Cocartesian, Vertical), where the vertical morphisms could be seen as “algebraic morphisms” with trivial geometric part, while the other were given respectively from the counit and unit of the codomain and domain sheaf along the direct and inverse images part of a geometric morphism; in some sense, algebraic data in the cartesian and cocartesian morphisms were trivial, so they could be seen as two sorts of “geometric morphisms” of modelled topoi. In the following we shall construct separately the spectrum of vertical, cartesian and cocartesian morphisms of modelled topoi, and define the spectrum of an arbitrary morphism as the composite of the spectrum of its vertical and cartesian part – or equivalently, its cocartesian and vertical part.

5.1
Functoriality relative to vertical morphisms

Recall the vertical morphisms are those of the form
(1_{\mathcal{F}},\phi):(\mathcal{F},F_{1})\rightarrow(\mathcal{F},F_{2})
, that is, whose underlying geometric morphism part is an identity – which makes the inverse and direct image part
\phi^{\flat}
and
\phi^{\sharp}
coincide. Vertical morphisms over
\mathcal{F}
correspond exactly to morphisms in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{F}]
, and we shall see the construction of the construction of their spectrum generalizes the set valued case which corresponded to vertical morphisms over
\mathcal{S}
.

5.1.1
.

For
\mathcal{F}
a Grothendieck topos, let
\phi:F_{1}\rightarrow F_{2}
be a morphism in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{F}]
. Then we are provided with a natural transformation of pseudofunctors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Cat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{*}}

defined at
c
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
as the pushout functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}(c)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}(c)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\phi_{c})_{*}n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{c})*}

whose naturality square at
u:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}(c_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}(c_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}(c_{1})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}(c_{1})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{c_{2}})*}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(s)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}(s)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{c_{1}})*}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

is given by the commutation of pushouts functors along the two sides of the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{F_{1}(c_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{F_{2}(c_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{F_{1}(c_{1})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{F_{2}(c_{1})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{c_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{c_{1}}}

By functoriality of the Grothendieck construction, that is, by naturality of oplaxcolimits, the natural transformation
\phi_{*}
induces a morphism of fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{F_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int\phi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{F_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{F_{2}}}

where
\int\phi_{*}(c,n)=(c,(\phi_{c})_{*}n)
for
(c,n)
in
\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}}
. Moreover, by composition of pushouts, each functor
(\phi_{c})_{*}
defines a morphism of lex site

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}(c)}^{{\rm op}},J_{F_{1}(c)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}(c)}^{{\rm op}},J_{F_{2}(c)})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{c})_{*}}

so that the induced functor
\int\phi_{*}
is a morphism of lex site. Hence
\int\phi_{*}
is a lex functor and is continuous for the jointly generated pretopologies
\langle\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}\iota_{c}(J_{F_{1}(c)})\rangle
and
\langle\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}\iota_{c}(J_{F_{2}(c)})\rangle
. Moreover, since identities are preserved by pushouts, we also have the following commutation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}(c)}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}(c)}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{c})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F_{2}}}

Hence
\int\phi_{*}
sends
\iota_{F_{1}}(J_{\mathcal{F}})
-families to
\iota_{F_{2}}(J_{\mathcal{F}})
-families. This provides us with a morphism of lex sites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}}^{{\rm op}},J_{F_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}}^{{\rm op}},J_{F_{2}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int\phi_{*}}

and consequently with a geometric morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi)}

5.1.2
.

Now, the associated morphisms of sheaves are defined as follows. For the direct image part, the data at each
(c,n)
of the map
n_{*}\phi_{c}
as obtained in the following pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{2}(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}((\phi_{c})_{*}n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{c})_{*}n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{*}\phi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

define altogether a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\phi)_{*}{\rm cod}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{\phi}}

which is sent after sheafification to a morphism in
\mathbb{T}[{\bf Spec}(F_{1})]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\phi)_{*}\widetilde{F_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F_{1}}}(\nu_{\phi})}

Hence we have to define

\widetilde{\phi}^{\sharp}=\mathfrak{a}_{J_{F_{1}}}(\nu_{\phi})

while the existence of its mate

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}\widetilde{F_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}}

is ensured through the adjunction defining
{\bf Spec}(\phi)
. However, it admits a more concrete construction, which is related to the (etale, locale) factorization in the sense that it is induced from taking the local part after precomposition with
\phi_{c}
, in the same vein as
proposition

2.5.6
:

Proposition 5.1.3
.

The morphism
\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}
is in
{\bf Loc}[{\bf Spec}(F_{2})]

Proof.

Recall that inverse images and sheafification commutes, so we can first compute the inverse image of the codomain functor over
\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}}
along
{\bf Spec}(\phi)
before sheafifying into
{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}\widetilde{F_{1}}
. Recall that the inverse image of the codomain functor (as a presheaf) is obtained as the left Kan extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int\phi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lan}}_{\int\phi_{*}}{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

For each
(c,n)
in
\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}}
, this left Kan extension is computed as the filtered colimit

({\textup{lan}}_{\int\phi_{*}}{\rm cod})(c,n)\simeq\underset{\int\phi_{*}\downarrow(c,n)}{{\textup{colim}}}{\rm cod}(m)

ranging over all
(u,f):(d,(\phi_{d})*m)\rightarrow(c,n)
where
(d,(\phi_{d})_{*}m)=\int\phi_{*}(d,m)
. Beware that we use there the native orientation of
\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}}
, so that this corresponds to an underlying morphism
u:c\rightarrow d
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
. But observe now that the subcategory of
\int\phi_{*}\downarrow(c,n)
admits a cofinal subcategory consisting of all the morphisms of the form
(1_{c},f):(c,(\phi_{c})_{*}m)\rightarrow(c,n)
, since any object in
\int\phi_{*}\downarrow(c,n)
factorizes in
\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}}
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d,(\phi_{c})_{*}m)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,F_{2}(u)_{*}(\phi_{c})_{*}m)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u,f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,1_{F_{2}(u)_{*}(\phi_{c})_{*}m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{c},\langle n,f\rangle)}

while any two parallel factorizations can be merged by a pushout.
Hence the colimit reduces to a colimit over those
(1_{c},f)
, so we can restrict in some sense at a computation in the fiber at
c
. But alike what was said at
proposition

2.5.6
, remark that
(\phi_{c})^{*}
is left adjoint to the precomposition functor
(\phi_{c})^{!}:\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}(c)}\rightarrow F_{1}(c)\downarrow\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, so that the cofinal category we exhibited above is equivalent to
\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}(c)}\downarrow n\phi_{c}
. Hence we have

{\textup{lan}}_{\int\phi_{*}}{\rm cod}(c,n)\simeq\underset{\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}(c)}\downarrow n\phi_{c}}{{\textup{colim}}}{\rm cod}(m)

From
proposition

1.1.5
, this exhibits
{\textup{lan}}_{\int\phi_{*}}{\rm cod}(c,n)
as the middle term in the (etale, locale) factorization of
n(\phi_{c})_{*}
, whose local part shall be denoted
(u_{\phi})_{c}:{\textup{lan}}_{\int\phi_{*}}{\rm cod}(c,n)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(c,n)
. Now by naturality this can be gathered into a local map in the category of
\mathbb{T}
-models in the presheaf topos
[\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}},\mathcal{S}]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}{\rm cod}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{f}}

whose sheafification returns
\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}
.
∎

Remark 5.1.4
.

Observe that we exploited morally the fact that (etale, local) factorizations in arbitrary topoi are done pointwisely (modulo sheafification) as observed in
proposition

1.1.11
, since we constructed the inverse image part from factorizations in the fibers before gathering them into a morphism of sheaf. From the triangle of geometric morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{F_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{F_{1}}}

we got a square in
{\bf Spec}(F_{2})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}h_{F_{1}}^{*}F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}\widetilde{wF_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h_{F_{2}}^{*}F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h_{F_{2}}^{*}F_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w\widetilde{F_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}\eta_{F_{1}}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F_{2}}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{F_{2}}^{*}\phi}

In fact, we can see this square encodes the (etale, locale) factorization of the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h_{F_{2}}^{*}F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w\widetilde{F_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{F_{2}}^{*}\phi\eta_{F_{2}}^{\flat}}

through a local unit, as
\widetilde{F_{2}}
is a local object,
\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}
is a local map, and
\eta_{F_{1}}^{\flat}
is the etale map coding the universal etale form under
F_{1}
.

5.2
Functoriality relative to horizontal morphisms

Recall that there are actually two kinds of horizontal morphisms, respectively the cartesian obtained through direct images and the cocartesian obtained through inverse images. We shall see it is sufficient to have only the cartesian together with the vertical ones to compute the spectrum of any morphism.

We turn to the cartesian morphisms and the construction of the spectrum of an arbitrary morphism. In fact the main difficulty is that one needs to consider the underlying geometric morphisms of a morphism of modelled topos as induced in a manner or another from a morphism of site in order to perform the pushout of basic etales map along it. Of course this is not possible in general to find simultaneously two
small
sites of presentation for the domain and codomain topos inducing a geometric morphism between them. But we can always induce it from a site morphism landing in the domain topos, which, together with its canonical topology, can be seen as a
small generated site
, for which there exist also far enough theory for our needs, as in
[
3
]
. We shall show we can extend canonically a
\mathbb{T}
-model seen as a sheaf of
\mathbb{T}
-objects over a presentation site to a sheaf over the whole topos with the canonical topology.

5.2.1
.

Recall, if
\mathcal{F}
admits a lex subcanonical site of presentation
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}},J_{\mathcal{F}})
then we have a geometric equivalence

{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}},J_{\mathcal{F}})\simeq{\bf Sh}({\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}},J_{\mathcal{F}}),J_{{\rm can}})

sending a sheaf
X
over
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
to its left Kan extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}^{{\rm op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\char 136\relax}^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}={\textup{lan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}^{{\rm op}}}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where
is full and faithful and lands in the category of sheaves as
J_{\mathcal{F}}
is subcanonical; in the other direction, a sheaf for the canonical topology is sent to its restriction along
{\text{\char 136\relax}}^{{\rm op}}
. Moreover, this construction extends to categories of sheaves in locally finitely presentable categories, in particular for
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
, so we have an equivalence of categories

\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{F}]\simeq\mathbb{T}[{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{F},J_{{\rm can}})]

5.2.2
.

Hence from the construction above, any
\mathbb{T}
-modelled topos
(\mathcal{F},F)
defines canonically another
\mathbb{T}
-modelled topos

({\bf Sh}(\mathcal{F},J_{{\rm can}}),\overline{F})
, which will be called its
extended modelled topos
, which is actually equipped with an invertible morphism of modelled topoi for
\mathcal{F}\simeq{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{F},J_{{\rm can}})
while
F\simeq{\textup{lan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}}F\circ\text{\char 136\relax}
(by subcanonicity) and
\overline{F}={\textup{lan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}}F
. Hence we expect those two modelled topoi to have the same spectrum, but from the construction of Coste sprectral site, this is not obvious – especially since we have not yet achieved functoriality. We shall in fact need this result in the following to prove functoriality for horizontal morphisms.

In fact, we can see that the spectral sites themselves of
F
and
\overline{F}
are not equivalent. At each
c
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have an isomorphism
F(c)\simeq\overline{F}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})
, and hence an equivalence between the fibers at object of
\mathcal{C}

\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}\simeq\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})}

This induces a canonical inclusion between the oplax colimits

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F}\simeq\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}}{{\textup{oplaxcolim}}}\,\mathcal{V}_{{F}(c)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{X\in\mathcal{F}}{{\textup{oplaxcolim}}}\,\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F}(X)}\simeq\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i}_{F}}

sending
(c,n)
to
(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c},n)
. However, though we have a dense inclusion
\text{\char 136\relax}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}
, the inclusion
i_{F}
is by no mean an equivalence itself, as the oplax colimits cannot be contracted – as a pseudocolimit may have been with an argument of cofinality. However, the following notion will help us to fix this at the level of induced topoi. Recall the following definition:

Definition 5.2.3
.

A morphism of site
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
is said to be
K
-dense
if it satisfies the following conditions:

-

f
creates covers, that is, a family
(u_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
is
J
-covering if and only if its image
(f(u_{i}))_{i\in I}
is a
K
-covering;

-

for any object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
there exists a
K
-cover of the form
(f_{i}:f(c_{i})\rightarrow d)_{i\in I}
with
c_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}
;

-

for any
c_{1},c_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
and
g:f(c_{1})\rightarrow f(c_{2})
, there exists a
J
-cover
(f_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
and a family of arrows
(g_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c_{2})_{i\in I}
such that
gf(f_{i})=f(g_{i})
.

Then it is known from
[
25
]
[Theorem 11.14](see also
[
3
]
[Remark 5.2]) that if
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
is a
K
-dense morphism of site, then the induced functor
{\bf Sh}(f)
is a geometric equivalence
{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)\simeq{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)
. Observe that in particular that, if
J
is subcanonical, then the dense inclusion
\text{\char 136\relax}:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow({\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J),J_{\rm can})
is a dense morphism of site, and moreover that restricting back the canonical topology to
\mathcal{C}
defines a dense morphism of sites
(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{C},J_{{\rm can}}\mid_{\mathcal{C}})
and also a dense morphism of site

(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\text{\char 136\relax}(\mathcal{C}),J_{{\rm can}}\mid_{\text{\char 136\relax}(\mathcal{C})})
.

Lemma 5.2.4
.

We have a geometric equivalence
{\bf Spec}(F)\simeq{\bf Spec}(\overline{F})
.

Proof.

We prove that the functor
{i}_{F}
above defines a
J_{\overline{F}}
-dense morphism of site

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}_{F}^{{\rm op}},J_{F})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{\overline{F}},J_{\overline{F}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i}_{F}}

For the first condition, it suffices to prove separately that
{i}_{F}
creates vertical and horizontal families:

-

for vertical families, observe that in each
c
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
, the fibers are equivalent as well as the topologies
J_{F(c)}
and
J_{\overline{F}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})}
: whence the creation of vertical families;

-

for horizontal families, this is a consequence of the fact that
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}},J_{\mathcal{F}})\rightarrow(\text{\char 136\relax}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}),J_{{\rm can}}\mid_{\text{\char 136\relax}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}})})
is a dense morphism of sites, so that a family
(\text{\char 136\relax}_{u_{i}}:\text{\char 136\relax}_{c_{i}}\rightarrow\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})_{i\in I}
is in
J_{{\rm can}}
if and only if
(u_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
is in
J_{\mathcal{F}}
. Hence a family
(\text{\char 136\relax}_{u_{i}},f):(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c_{i}},n_{i})\rightarrow(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c},n))_{i\in I}
is
J_{\overline{F}}
-covering if and only if
({u_{i}},f):({c_{i}},n_{i})\rightarrow(c,n))_{i\in I}
is
J_{F}
-covering.

For the second condition, recall that
is a dense functor so that for any
X
in
\mathcal{F}
we have a colimit
X\simeq\text{\char 136\relax}\downarrow X
(where beware the colimit is computed in the category of sheaves). Hence
\text{\char 136\relax}\downarrow X
is never empty, and in fact, from the definition of colimits in topoi and their relation with the canonical topology, the family
(a:\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}\rightarrow X)_{\text{\char 136\relax}\downarrow X}
is a cover in
J_{\rm can}
. Hence one can take for each object
(X,n)
of
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F}}
with
n:\overline{F}(X)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
the horizontal
J_{\overline{F}}
-cover

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c},\overline{F}(a)_{*}n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X,n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a,n_{*}\overline{F}(a))}

where
(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c},\overline{F}(a)_{*}n)\simeq{i}_{F}(c,\overline{F}(a)_{*}n)
.

For the third item, we use that, by subcanonicity of
J_{\mathcal{F}}
,
is full and faithful, so that for any
(c_{1},n_{1})
,
(c_{2},n_{2})
and
(f,g):(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c_{1}},n_{1})\rightarrow(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c_{2}},n_{2})
, the arrow
f
must come from some
f=\text{\char 136\relax}_{u}
with
u:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
and hence we had already
(u,g):(c_{1},n_{1})\rightarrow(c_{2},n_{2})
.
∎

5.2.5
.

We saw that
(\mathcal{F},F)
is actually equivalent as a modelled topos to its extended form
({\bf Sh}(\mathcal{F}_{2},J_{can}),\overline{F})
. Consequently, we expect their respective spectrum to be equivalent not only at the level of the underlying topoi but at the level of the structure sheaves. This can be seen as follows: at each
(c,n)
of
\mathcal{V}_{F}
we have
i_{F}(c,n)=(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c},n)
as
\overline{F}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})=F(c)
– so that
n
is both an object of
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})}
; then we have a pointwise equality
i_{F*}{\rm cod}(c,n)={\rm cod}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c},n)=n
, so that we get an isomorphism after sheafification relative to
J_{\overline{F}}

\widetilde{\overline{F}}\simeq{\bf Sh}(i_{F})_{*}\widetilde{F}

whose mate between inverse images is also an isomorphism as the unit and counit of the adjoint equivalence
{\bf Sh}(i_{F})^{*}\dashv{\bf Sh}(i_{F})_{*}
are so.

5.2.6
.

Now for a morphism
(f,\phi):(\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})\rightarrow(\mathcal{F}_{2},F_{2})
, even though
f
does not necessarily arise form a morphism of site
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}_{1}},J_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}_{2}},J_{\mathcal{F}_{2}})
,
f
is still induced as a morphism of sites
f^{*}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{2}
sending
J_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}
-covers to covers for the canonical topology
J_{{\rm can}}
on
\mathcal{F}_{2}
seen as a small-generated standard site, and the direct image can be computed explicitly at
c
in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
as

f_{*}F_{2}(c)\simeq\overline{F_{2}}(f^{*}(c))

For this later is an ordinary set-based
\mathbb{T}
-model, we can compute its spectral site
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F_{2}}(f^{*}(c))}
, and for each
c
we have a transition functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}(c)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F_{2}}(f^{*}(c))}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi^{\sharp}_{c})_{*}}

which induces by naturality of the oplax colimit a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{F_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}}{{\textup{oplaxcolim}}}\,\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F_{2}}(f^{*}(c))}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int\phi^{\sharp}_{*}}

where the later oplax colimit is
\mathcal{V}_{f_{*}F_{2}}
. Then we can apply
5.1.1
to the vertical morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{(}\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F}_{1},f_{*}F_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{\mathcal{F}_{1}},(\phi^{\sharp},\phi^{\sharp}))}

so we get in particular a geometric morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(f_{*}F_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi^{\sharp})}

5.2.7
.

On the other hand, for each
f^{*}(c)
is in particular an object of the sheaf topos
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, reindexing along
f^{*}:\mathcal{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{2}
induces a canonical inclusion between the oplax colimits

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}}{{\textup{oplaxcolim}}}\,\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F_{2}}(f^{*}(c))}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underset{X\in\mathcal{F}_{2}}{{\textup{oplaxcolim}}}\,\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F_{2}}(X)}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{(f,\phi)}}

which is moreover trivially a morphism of sites. Hence it induces a geometric morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\overline{F}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(f_{*}F_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(q_{(f,\phi)})}

Hence from
lemma

5.2.4
, we can finally compose all those data into a geometric morphism
{\bf Spec}(\phi)
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\overline{F}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(f_{*}F_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(q_{(f,\phi)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}({i}_{F_{2}})\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi^{\sharp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi)}

5.2.8
.

We conclude with the computation of the sheaf data associated to
{\bf Spec}(\phi)
. This will be done by combining easy to compute data associated to each part of the decomposition
{\bf Spec}(\phi)={\bf Spec}(\phi^{\sharp}){\bf Sh}(q_{(f,\phi)}){\bf Sh}(i_{F_{2}})
.

From
5.2.5
we know that
\widetilde{\overline{F_{2}}}\simeq{\bf Sh}(i_{F_{2}})_{*}\widetilde{F_{2}}
. On the other side, the vertical morphism
(1,\phi^{\sharp}):(\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})\rightarrow(\mathcal{F}_{1},f_{*}F_{2})
is sent to a morphism of locally modelled topos

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(F_{1}),\widetilde{F_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(f_{*}F_{2}),\widetilde{f_{*}F_{2}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Spec}(\phi),\widetilde{\phi})}

We are going to see that this part contains actually all the sheaf information of the composite above.

Indeed, the intermediate part acts like restriction, that is,

\widetilde{f_{*}F_{2}}\simeq{\bf Sh}(q_{f,\phi*})\overline{F_{2}}

This is because
\mathcal{V}_{f_{*}F_{2}}
is a subcategory of
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{F}_{2}}
with the
\mathcal{V}_{F_{2}(f^{*}(c))}
as fibers, and again, one can apply sheafification to the equality between codomain functors. This returns a cartesian morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(f_{*}F_{2}),\widetilde{f_{*}F_{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(\overline{F_{2}}),\widetilde{\overline{F_{2}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Sh}(q_{(f,\phi)},1_{\widetilde{f_{*}F_{2}}})}

Hence we can define the direct image part
\widetilde{\phi}^{\sharp}
of
\widetilde{\phi}
as the composite in
{\bf Spec}(F_{1})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{f_{*}F_{2}}\simeq{\bf Sh}(q_{(f,\phi)})_{*}\widetilde{\overline{F_{2}}}\simeq{\bf Spec}(\phi)_{*}F_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi^{\sharp}}}

Summing up those considerations, we have a decomposition in
\mathbb{T}_{J}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}^{\bf Loc}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(F_{1}),\widetilde{F_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(F_{2}),\widetilde{F_{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{({\bf Spec}(f_{*}F_{2}),\widetilde{f_{*}F_{2}})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{({\bf Spec}(\overline{F_{2}}),\widetilde{\overline{F_{2}}})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Sh}(q_{(f,\phi)},1_{\widetilde{f_{*}F_{2}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Spec}(\phi),\widetilde{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Spec}(\phi),\widetilde{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Sh}(i_{F_{2}}),1_{{\bf Sh}(i_{F_{2}})_{*}\widetilde{F_{2}}})}

5.3
Spectrum of 2-cells

For the sake of exhaustiveness, we give here to the treatment of 2-cells.

5.3.1
.

Take a 2-cell in
\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F}_{2},F_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

that is, a 2-cell in
{\bf GTop}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

such that we have one, hence both of the following commutations between inverse and direct images in
\mathcal{F}_{2}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
respectively respectively

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\flat}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\flat}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g_{*}F_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{*}F_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\sharp}_{F_{2}}}

The later, as a triangle of vertical morphisms of modelled topoi, induces an invertible 2-cell between the corresponding spectra

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(g_{*}F_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(f_{*}F_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\gamma^{\sharp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi^{\sharp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\sigma^{\sharp}_{F_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

which induces in
{\bf Spec}(F_{1})
a triangle toward direct images

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\gamma^{\sharp})_{*}\widetilde{g_{*}F_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\phi^{\sharp})_{*}\widetilde{f_{*}F_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi^{\sharp}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\gamma^{\sharp}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\phi^{\sharp})(\widetilde{\sigma^{\sharp}_{F_{2}}}^{\sharp}})}

where the middle arrow comes from the direct image part of
\widetilde{\sigma^{\sharp}_{F_{2}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{g_{*}F_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\sigma^{\sharp}_{F_{2}})_{*}(\widetilde{f_{*}F_{2})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\sigma^{\sharp}_{F_{2}}}^{\sharp}}

5.3.2
.

Now recall that, though neither
f
nor
g
are supposed to be induced from a morphism of site, we can use the extended sheaf
\overline{F_{2}}
associated to
F_{2}
which is at each
c
of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}
respectively
g_{*}F_{2}(c)=\overline{F_{2}}(g^{*}(c))
and
f_{*}F_{2}(c)=\overline{F_{2}}(f^{*}(c))
. Then the component at
c
of the direct image part
\sigma^{\sharp}_{F_{2}}
is also the image along
\overline{F_{2}}
of the component of
\sigma^{\sharp}:g_{*}\Rightarrow f_{*}
at
c
by Yoneda lemma, that is

(\sigma^{\sharp}_{F_{2}})_{c}=\overline{F_{2}}(\sigma^{\sharp}_{c})

along which we can consider the pushout functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{F_{2}}(g^{*}(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{F_{2}}(f^{*}(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(\overline{F_{2}}(\sigma^{\sharp}_{c})_{*}n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F_{2}}(\sigma^{\sharp}_{c})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F_{2}}(\sigma^{\sharp}_{c})_{*}n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{*}\overline{F_{2}}(\sigma^{\sharp}_{c})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This defines at each
c
a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{\overline{F_{2}}(g^{*}(c))}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{\overline{F_{2}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{\overline{F_{2}}(f^{*}(c))}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{{\rm op}}_{g^{*}(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{{\rm op}}_{f^{*}(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F_{2}}(\sigma^{\sharp}_{c})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\sigma^{\sharp}_{c}})^{{\rm op}}}

Those data induce a morphism of fibrations between the fibered sites of the inverse images

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{g_{*}\overline{F_{2}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{\overline{F_{2}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}^{{\rm op}}_{f_{*}\overline{F_{2}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{(g,\gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{(f,\phi)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int\overline{F_{2}}(\sigma^{\sharp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\sigma^{\sharp}})^{{\rm op}}}

5.3.3
.

Then to get the desired 2-cell in
\mathbb{T}_{J}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}^{\bf Loc}
, one has to paste the associated 2-cell between the spectra of inverse images together with the spectrum of the triangle of vertical morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(g_{*}F_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\overline{F_{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(f_{*}F_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\gamma^{\sharp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi^{\sharp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\sigma^{\sharp}_{F_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(q_{(g,\gamma)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(q_{(f,\phi)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(i_{F_{2}})\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\sigma)}

We would have to check that this 2-cell satisfies the compatibility condition of morphisms of modelled topos. But this comes from the very definition of
{\bf Spec}(\sigma)
as induced from
\overline{\sigma^{\sharp}}
so that its component at
{\bf Sh}(i_{F_{2}})_{*}\widetilde{F_{2}}
satisfies

{\bf Spec}(\sigma)_{{\bf Sh}(i_{F_{2}})_{*}\widetilde{F_{2}}}=\widetilde{\sigma^{\sharp}_{F_{2}}}^{\sharp}

This achieves to produce a 2-cell of locally modelled topoi

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(F_{1}),\widetilde{F_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(F_{2}),\widetilde{F_{2}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Spec}(\phi),\widetilde{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Spec}(\gamma),\widetilde{\gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\sigma)}

5.3.4
.

To sum up this section, we have shown how to make the spectrum into a pseudofunctor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}_{J}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}^{\bf Loc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}}

The next section will now prove it to be the desired left adjoint of the inclusion
\iota_{J,{\bf Loc}}
.

6
Site-theoretic version of the spectral adjunction

In this section we shall prove that the notion of spectrum as constructed in this chapter has the desired universal property announced in the previous chapter and is part of the desired spectral adjunction. In
[
6
]
, there was already such a proof, yet we shall follow a different strategy based on the in-depth description of the spectral site and some reflections on locally modelled topoi.

An important preliminary result of this section is the localness of the spectrum of a local object over its base topos, generalizing
proposition

2.3.7
. We also describe how the spectrum operates relative to local units and stalks of its input sheaf of
\mathbb{T}
-models.

6.1
Spectra of locally modelled topoi are local over their base

6.1.1
.

Consider now a
\mathbb{T}_{J}
-locally modelled topos
(\mathcal{E},E)
with
\mathcal{E}={\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}},J_{\mathcal{E}})
. Recall that the condition that
E
is a
J
-local objects in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
amounts to saying that the corresponding
J
-continuous functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{*}}

sends any
J
-family
([\theta_{j}]_{\mathbb{T}}:\{\overline{x_{j}},\phi_{j}\}\rightarrow\{\overline{x},\phi\})_{j\in I}
to an epimorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle{\coprod_{j\in I}}E(\{\overline{x_{j}},\phi_{j}\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(\{\overline{x},\phi\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle E([\theta_{i}]_{\mathbb{T}})\rangle_{j\in I}}

But the property of being an epimorphism in the category of sheaves over
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}},J_{\mathcal{E}})
says that for any
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
and any
a\in E(\{\overline{x},\phi\})(c)
, there is a
J_{\mathcal{E}}
-cover
(u_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I^{\prime}}
such that the image
E(\{\overline{x},\phi\})(u_{i})(a)
is in the range of
\langle E([\theta_{j}]_{\mathbb{T}})\rangle_{j\in I}(c_{i})
. But each
E(c)
is a
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
-model, and we have

E(\{\overline{x},\phi\})(c)\simeq\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}](K_{\phi},E(c))

so
a
is some
a:K_{\phi}\rightarrow E(c)
. Then this means that each of the composite
E(u_{i})a
factorizes through some
f_{\theta_{j}}:K_{\phi}\rightarrow K_{\phi_{j}}
for some
j\in J
, and then we have a factorization of the identiy of
E(c_{i})
through the corresponding pushout as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{\phi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(c_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(c_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{\phi_{j}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{*}K_{\phi_{j}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(u_{i})_{*}a_{*}K_{\phi_{j}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(u_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\theta_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}f_{\theta_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(u_{i})_{*}a_{*}f_{\theta_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This leads to the following:

Proposition 6.1.2
.

If
E
is a
J
-local object in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
, then
\iota_{wE}:(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}},J_{\mathcal{E}})\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{V}_{wE}^{{\rm op}},J_{wE})
is also a comorphism of site, and defines a geometric morphism
e_{E}={\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})_{*}\dashv{\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})^{!}
which is moreover a section of
h_{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{E}}

Proof.

For any
c
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
, a
\iota_{c}(J_{F(c)})
-cover
(1_{c},m_{j}):(c,n_{j})\rightarrow(c,1_{F(c)}))_{j\in J}
coming from
(m_{j}:E(c)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n_{j}))_{j\in I}
is obtained as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{\phi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{\phi_{j}}}}
