{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\theta_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

with
([\theta_{j}]_{\mathbb{T}}:\{\overline{x_{j}},\phi_{j}\}\rightarrow\{\overline{x},\phi\})_{j\in I}
a
J
-family. But from what precedes, as
a
is an element of
E(\{\overline{x},\phi\})(c)
, there is some
(u_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I^{\prime}}
such that for each
i\in I^{\prime}
, there is
j\in I
and a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{\phi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(c_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{\phi_{j}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(c_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\theta_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(u_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

In other word, for any
i\in I^{\prime}
there is some
j\in I
and a factorization in
\mathcal{V}_{wE}^{{\rm op}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,n_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c_{i},1_{E(c_{i})})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,1_{F(c)})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{c},n_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u_{i},E(u_{i}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u_{i},b)}

But in the diagram above,
(u_{i},E(u_{i}))_{i\in I^{\prime}}
is an horizontal family in
\iota_{wE}(J_{\mathcal{E}})
, while
(1_{c},n_{i})
is a vertical family in
\iota_{c}(J_{F(c)})
refining it; in particular, the vertical family must hence be contained in the sieve generated by the horizontal family, and so is the sieve its generates. Hence any
J_{\mathcal{V}_{E(-)}}
-covering sieve is in particular a
\iota_{wE}(J_{\mathcal{E}})
-covering sieve: the topology
J_{\mathcal{V}_{E(-)}}
is coarser than
\iota_{wE}(J_{\mathcal{E}})
, and hence the inclusion
\iota_{wE}
becomes a comorphism of sites. ∎

Now recall that in the case of set-based models, the spectrum of a local object was a local topos: this result will generalize as follows. To this end we invoke
[
3
]
[Theorem 7.20] characterization of local geometric morphisms: a continuous comorphism of site
F:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
which is moreover a morphism of site induces a triple of adjoints
{\bf Sh}(F)^{*}\dashv{\bf Sh}(F)_{*}\dashv{\bf Sh}(F)^{!}
, which can be proven to be moreover local if
F
is full and faithful.

Remark 6.1.3
.

Actually,
[
3
]
[Theorem 7.20, (iii)] requires the functor
F
to be
J
-full and
J
-faithful
for the condition to hold. Though we cannot give the definition of those notions which involves a notion of local equality of morphisms in the sheaf topos, we have in
[
3
]
[Proposition 5.16] the following characterization:
F
is
J
-full and
J
-faithul if and only if for each
c
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have an isomorphism

\mathfrak{a}_{J}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{J}(\mathcal{D}[F(-),F(c)])

But then observe that if
F
is full and faithful in the usual sense, then for each
d
we have
\mathcal{C}[d,c]\simeq\mathcal{D}[F(d),F(c)]
, and hence there is a natural isomorphism
\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}\simeq\mathcal{D}[F(-),F(c)]
which still is an isomorphism after sheafification, so that
F
is also
J
-full and
J
-faithful.

Corollary 6.1.4
.

If
(\mathcal{E},E)
is a
\mathbb{T}_{J}
-locally modelled topos, then
h_{wE}:{\bf Spec}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is a local geometric morphism, with center
e_{E}
. In particular, whenever
\mathcal{E}
was itself a local topos with center
x
, then
{\bf Spec}(E)
is a local topos with center
e_{E}x
.

Proof.

We saw in the previous part that
\iota_{wE}
becomes in this case a continuous comorphism, while still being also a morphism of site: hence it induces a triple of adjoints functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(wE)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})^{!}}

Moreover, as
\iota_{E}
is full and faithful, then by the remark above, this triple of adjoints functors defines a local geometric morphism. Observe that we have the equalities

\underset{h^{*}_{wE}}{\underbrace{{\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})^{*}={\bf Sh}(p_{wE})_{!}}}\dashv\underset{h_{wE*}=e^{*}_{E}}{\underbrace{{\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})_{*}={\bf Sh}(p_{wE})^{*}}}\dashv\underset{e_{E*}}{\underbrace{{\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})^{!}={\bf Sh}(p_{wE})_{*}}}

where both
h^{*}_{E}
and
e_{E*}
are full and faithful. In particular
h_{E}
is a connected geometric morphism.
∎

6.1.5
.

Now our goal is to complete the geometric morphism
e_{E}
with a local map
\epsilon^{\flat}_{E}:e_{E}^{*}\widetilde{wE}\rightarrow E
in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
defining a retraction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{E}^{*}\widetilde{wE}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces wE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces wE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces we^{*}_{E}\eta^{\flat}_{wE}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{\flat}_{E}}

(where we used the fact that
E\simeq e^{*}_{E}h^{*}_{wE}E\simeq{\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})_{*}{\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})^{*}E
from the fact that
{\bf Sh}(\iota_{wE})^{*}
is full and faithful, hence has invertible unit). But we can already guess that actually such a map
\epsilon^{\flat}_{E}
has to be an isomorphism: indeed, we know from
proposition

4.4.8
that
\eta^{\flat}_{wE}
has to be an etale map, as well as its inverse image
e^{*}_{E}(\eta^{\flat}_{wE})
, while
\epsilon^{\flat}_{E}
must be defined in such a way it is local: but as we hence have a factorization of the identity of
E
, those maps form an (etale, local) factorization of the identity, hence both are isomorphisms, and we then use that
w
is conservative.

Actually, we can see directly which is this isomorphism: recall that sheafification commutes with inverse images, so that we have

\displaystyle e^{*}_{E}\widetilde{wE}

\displaystyle\simeq e^{*}_{E}\mathfrak{a}_{J}{\rm cod}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{J}{\rm cod}\circ\iota_{E}

But we have at each
c
of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
that
{\rm cod}(\iota_{E}(c))={\rm cod}(c,1_{E(c)})=E(c)
: so we have a pointwise equality
{\rm cod}\circ\iota_{E}=E
which is sent to an isomorphism after sheafification
\epsilon^{\sharp}_{E}:we^{*}_{E}\widetilde{wE}\simeq E
for
\mathfrak{a}_{J}\iota_{\mathcal{E}}E\simeq E
as
E
is already a sheaf.

Now the mate
\epsilon^{\sharp}_{E}
coincides up to a canonical iso with the unit of the
e^{*}_{E}\dashv e_{E*}
adjunction as one has

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{wE}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{E*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{E*}e^{*}_{E}\widetilde{wE}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{\sharp}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{*}(\epsilon^{\flat}_{E})\atop\simeq}

But as
h_{wE}
is a local geometric geometric morphism with
h_{wE}^{!}\simeq e_{E*}
,
e_{E*}
is full and faithful, so its unit is an isomorphism, and as a consequence
\epsilon^{\sharp}_{E}
is itself an isomorphism.

This exhibits the counit as an horizontal morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(wE),\widetilde{wE})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{E},E)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e_{E},(1_{E},1_{\widetilde{wE}}))}

Remark 6.1.6
.

Beware that
e_{E*}
is not just precomposition with the projection
p_{wE}
as one needs further sheafification over the vertical families.

Remark 6.1.7
.

When
E
lives in
\mathcal{S}
, then
(\mathcal{S},E)
being a locally modelled topos means for
E
to be a set-valued local object. Then
e_{E}
defines an initial point of
{\bf Spec}(wE)
, and as
e^{*}(*)=1_{{\bf Spec}(E)}
one has

\displaystyle\Gamma\widetilde{wE}

\displaystyle\simeq\widetilde{wE}(1_{E})

\displaystyle\simeq h_{wE*}E

\displaystyle\simeq e^{*}_{E}\widetilde{wE}

Therefor
e_{E}
corresponds to the local form
1_{E}:E\rightarrow E
and we have a representation

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\simeq e_{E}^{*}\widetilde{wE}

\displaystyle\simeq\Gamma\widetilde{wE}

In the case of a set based local object, it would just express the fact that global sections are determined by the stalk at the focal point of the spectrum of the local object. The general case means the same thing but in term of
\mathcal{E}
-indexed point, and enforces that the inverse image of the structure sheaf along
e^{*}_{E}
– equivalently, the direct image along
h_{E*}
– returns the original sheaf. Hence local objects enjoy sheaf representation “for free” – which however will require additional assumption for arbitrary objects.

6.2
The spectral bi-adjunction

In this subsection we prove the morphisms of modelled topos
(h_{F},\eta_{F})
and the morphisms of locally modelled topos
(e_{E},\epsilon_{E})
are respectively units and counits of a bi-adjunction
{\bf Spec}\dashv\iota_{J,{\bf Loc}}
. Our proof here is totally different from
[
6
]
– as it exploits the concrete site presentation of the spectrum as well as the localness result established above.

Theorem 6.2.1
.

We have a biadjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}_{J}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}^{{\bf Loc}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{J,{\bf Loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{F},F)
be a
\mathbb{T}
-modelled topos and
(\mathcal{E},E)
a
\mathbb{T}_{J}
-locally modelled topos. We must construct an equivalence of homcategories

{\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}}\big[(\mathcal{F},F),(\mathcal{E},wE)\big]\simeq{\mathbb{T}_{J}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}^{{\bf Loc}}}\big[({\bf Spec}(F),\widetilde{F}),(\mathcal{E},E)\big]

Let
(f,\phi):(\mathcal{F},F)\rightarrow(\mathcal{E},E)
be in
{\mathbb{T}\mbox{-}{\bf GTop}}
. Then by pseudonaturality of
(h,\eta):1\Rightarrow\iota_{J,{\bf Loc}}{\bf Spec}
we have a pseudocommutative square in
{\bf GTop}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(wE)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{wE}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{f}\atop\simeq}

and in particular a natural isomorphism between inverse images

{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}h_{F}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{f}}}{{\simeq}}h_{wE}^{*}f^{*}

Hence we have a commutative square between inverse images in
{\bf Spec}(wE)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}h_{F}^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h_{wE}^{*}f^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{*}_{wE}wE}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}w\widetilde{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w\widetilde{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{wE}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{wE}^{*}\phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}\eta_{F}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta_{f})_{F}\atop\simeq}

Pasting the diagram of geometric morphisms above with the retraction we obtained at
proposition

6.1.2
, we get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(wE)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Spec}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{wE}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Spec}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{f}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

so that, from
e^{*}_{E}h_{wE}^{*}\simeq 1
, the commutative square of inverse images above is sent to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces wE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{E}^{*}{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}w\widetilde{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{E}^{*}w\widetilde{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{*}_{E}\eta_{wE}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{E}^{*}w\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{E}^{*}{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}\eta_{F}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}}

But from
6.1.5
we know that
e^{*}_{E}\eta_{wE}^{\flat}
is an isomorphism with inverse
\epsilon^{\flat}_{E}
as
E
is local, so this actually defines a morphism in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{E}]
between local objects

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{E}^{*}{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}w\widetilde{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces wE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{\flat}_{E}e_{E}^{*}w\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}}

But as
\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}
is local as well as the isomorphism
\epsilon^{\flat}_{E}
this morphism is local, and comes uniquely from a morphism in
\mathbb{T}_{J}[\mathcal{E}]^{\bf Loc}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{E}^{*}{\bf Spec}(\phi)^{*}\widetilde{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{\flat}_{E}e_{E}^{*}\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}}

This provides us with a morphism of locally modelled topoi which satisfies by its very construction the following pseudocommutation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F},F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{E},E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(F),\widetilde{F})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Spec}(\phi)e_{E},{\epsilon^{\flat}_{E}e_{E}^{*}\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{F},\eta_{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

The converse direction follow similar argument. A morphism of locally modelled topoi

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(F),\widetilde{F})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{E},E)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,\gamma)}

can have its image pasted with with the unit of
(\mathcal{F},F)
to get a morphism as desired; this will return in
\mathcal{E}
a composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}h_{F}^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}w\widetilde{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces wE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(\eta_{F}^{\flat})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\flat}(\eta^{\flat}_{F})}

which is actually uniquely determined from
\eta_{F}^{\flat}
and
\gamma^{\flat}
as the first one is etale – as well as its inverse image – while the second one is local. Hence no other morphism
g^{*}F\rightarrow E
induces the same composite as it would provide two distinct local parts for a same map, which is impossible by the uniqueness of the factorization. Proving that
(g,\gamma)(h_{F},\eta_{F})
induces back the same
(g,\gamma)
after applying the spectrum and pasting it with the canonical retraction of
(\mathcal{E},E)
is routine.
∎

Remark 6.2.2
.

Observe that in both directions, we actually compute the etale-local factorization of the inverse image of the morphism of sheaves, where the etale part is indexed by the unit
\eta^{\flat}_{F}
of
F
in a universal way, in the sense that
f^{*}\eta^{\flat}_{F}
still indexes the etale parts of morphisms from
f^{*}F
toward local objects in
\mathcal{E}
.

6.2.3
.

To conclude this section, we shall use the result above to describe points of
{\bf Spec}(F)
and how they are related to points of the underlying topos
\mathcal{F}
. Observe that each point
x:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
induces a stalk
x^{*}F
which is in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
. From the general theory of sheaves, we know this stalk to be expressed as the filtered colimit

x^{*}F\simeq\underset{(c,a)\in(\int x^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,F(c)

where the colimit inclusions are the restrictions functors
\rho^{(c,a)}_{x}:F(c)\rightarrow x^{*}F
– this colimit being filtered for
x^{*}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is flat. Moreover,
x
determines a morphism of modelled topoi

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F},F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{S},x^{*}F)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x,1_{x^{*}F})}

which is sent to a morphism of locally modelled topoi

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(F),\widetilde{F})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(x^{*}F),\widetilde{x^{*}F})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Spec}(1_{x^{*}F}),\widetilde{1_{x^{*}F}})}

Theorem 6.2.4
.

For any
x:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
we have a pseudolimit decomposition

{\bf Spec}(x^{*}F)\simeq\underset{(c,a)\in\int x^{*}}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,{\bf Spec}(F(c))

Proof.

From the bi-adjunction obtained in
theorem

2.7.2
, we know that
{\bf Spec}
preserves bicolimits of set-valued models. Hence we have

{\bf Spec}(x^{*}F,\widetilde{x^{*}F})\simeq\underset{(c,a)\in\int x^{*}}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,({\bf Spec}(F(c)),\widetilde{F(c)})

Now from the expression of colimits of modelled topoi, and the fact that
\iota_{J,{\bf Loc}}
is a morphism of fibration, we know that the underlying Grothendieck topos is a cofiltered bilimit as desired.
∎

6.3
Points of the spectrum

6.3.1
.

Now we would like to describe points of
{\bf Spec}(F)
and how they are related to points of the underlying topos
\mathcal{F}
. Observe that each point
x:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
induces a stalk
x^{*}F
which is in
\mathbb{T}[\mathcal{S}]
. From the general theory of sheaves, we know this stalk to be expressed as the filtered colimit

x^{*}F\simeq\underset{(c,a)\in(\int x^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,F(c)

where the colimit inclusions are the restrictions functors
\rho^{(c,a)}_{x}:F(c)\rightarrow x^{*}F
– this colimit being filtered as
x^{*}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is flat. Moreover,
x
determine a morphism of modelled topos

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{F},F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{S},x^{*}F)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x,1_{x^{*}F})}

which is sent to a morphism of locally modelled topoi

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(F),\widetilde{F})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Spec}(x^{*}F),\widetilde{x^{*}F})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\bf Spec}(1_{x^{*}F}),\widetilde{1_{x^{*}F}})}

Theorem 6.3.2
.

For any
x:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
we have a pseudolimit decomposition

{\bf Spec}(x^{*}F)\simeq\underset{(c,a)\in\int x^{*}}{\lim}\,{\bf Spec}(F(c))

Proof.

To see this we are going to prove the pseudocolimit decomposition

\mathcal{V}_{x^{*}F}\simeq\underset{(c,a)\in(\int x^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}

Observe that we have a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(c,a)\in\int x^{*}}{{\textup{oplaxcolim}}}\,\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}_{x^{*}F}}}

sending an object
((c,a),n)
with
n:F(c)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(n)
and
a\in x^{*}(c)
to the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rho^{(c,a)}_{x}}_{*}{\rm cod}(n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c,a)}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho^{(c,a)}_{x}}_{*}n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

and a morphism
(u,f):((c_{1},a_{1}),n_{1})\rightarrow((c_{2},a_{2}),n_{2})
of
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{(c,a)\in(\int x^{*})^{{\rm op}}}\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
to the morphism induced from the universal property of the pushout as depicted below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rho^{(c_{2},a_{2})}_{x}}_{*}B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}_{*}B_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c_{2},a_{2})}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho^{(c_{2},a_{2})}_{x}}_{*}n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}_{*}n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This functor is essentially surjective: indeed, for any pushout square as below in
\mathcal{V}_{x^{*}F}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{*}K^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{*}m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

then because of the colimit decomposition of
x^{*}F={\textup{colim}}_{(c,a)\in\int x^{*}}F(c)
we have a lift of
b
through some
\rho^{(c,a)}_{x}
which induces a factorization of pushouts a follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{b}_{*}K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{*}K^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c,a)}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Now recall that a morphism
(u,f):((c_{1},a_{1}),n_{1})\rightarrow((c_{2},a_{2}),n_{2})
is cartesian if
f
if and only if the corresponding square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pushout. Then any cartesian morphism
(u,f):((c_{1},a_{1}),n_{1})\rightarrow((c_{2},a_{2}),n_{2})
is obviously sent to an isomorphism in
\mathcal{V}_{x^{*}F}
by composition and uniqueness of pushouts. Hence the functor from the oplax colimit localizes cartesian morphisms, and hence factorizes through the pseudocolimit as an essentially surjective functor

\underset{(c,a)\in(\int x^{*})^{{\rm op}}}{{\textup{colim}}}\,\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{x^{*}F}

Now we must prove that this functor is full and faithful. For faithfulness, observe that for any parallel pair in the oplax colimit which are send to the same morphism in
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
as depicted below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rho^{(c_{2},a_{2})}_{x}}_{*}B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}_{*}B_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c_{2},a_{2})}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho^{(c_{2},a_{2})}_{x}}_{*}n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}_{*}n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

there exists by filteredness of
(\int x^{*})^{{\rm op}}
some
(c,a)
and a morphism
w:c\rightarrow c_{1}
equalizing
u
,
v
and such that we have equalization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{*}F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c_{2},a_{2})}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(w)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c,a)}_{x}}

Observe moreover that
f
,
g
are uniquely determined by the induced maps
\langle f,n_{1}\rangle
and
\langle g,n_{1}\rangle
respectively as depicted below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(u)_{*}B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(v)_{*}B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(u)_{*}n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(v)_{*}n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle f,n_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle g,n_{1}\rangle}

But we have
F(w)_{*}(F(u)_{*}n_{2})=F(w)_{*}(F(v)_{*}n_{2})
, so that we get a parallel pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(w)_{*}F(u)_{*}B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(w)_{*}B_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(w)_{*}F(u)_{*}n_{2}\atop=F(w)_{*}F(v)_{*}n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(w)_{*}n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle f,F(w)_{*}n_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle g,F(w)_{*}n_{1}\rangle}

But those two arrows are induced actually from a same arrow

{n_{1}}_{*}F(w)\langle f,F(w)_{*}n_{1}\rangle={n_{1}}_{*}F(w)\langle g,F(w)_{*}n_{1}\rangle

and hence are equal. Hence, being equalized by a cartesian morphism
(w,{n_{1}}_{*}F(w))
,
(u,f)
and
(v,g)
are already identified in the pseudocolimit: hence the faithfulness.

For fullness, consider
((c_{1},a_{1}),n_{1})
and
((c_{2},a_{2}),n_{2})
and a morphism
f
between their image as in the situation below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rho^{(c_{2},a_{2})}_{x}}_{*}B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}_{*}B_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c_{2},a_{2})}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho^{(c_{2},a_{2})}_{x}}_{*}n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}_{*}n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Then by filteredness of

there is some

,

, so that we have a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(c)}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(u_{1})}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{(c,a)}_{x}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho^{(c_{1},a_{1})}_{x}}_{*}n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

so that we can infere the existence of some arrow

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
\mathcal{V}_{F(c)}
inducing
f
in
\mathcal{V}_{x^{*}F}
. Hence the fullness.

Now the pseudocolimit is equipped with an induced topology

\langle\bigcup_{(c,a)\in\int x^{*}}J_{F(c)}\rangle

and one would have to prove that any
J_{x^{*}F}
-covering family is a covering family of this induced topology and conversely: but the strategy is exactly the same as in
theorem

2.2.7
, so we dispense ourselves from this redundant work. We end up with an equivalence of sites, and hence, with an equivalence of the induced topoi as desired.
∎

7
Naturality of the spectral adjunction

For the sake of completeness, we treat also here the functoriality of the spectral construction relative to transformations of geometries as described at
definition

1.3.1
. In fact most of the geometries presented in the examples section are related to each other through such transformations, which induce such comparisons functor – some of them where individually investigated, as the so called
Belluce functor
we recover as a comparison map at
remark

8.6.7
.

7.1
Comparison of spectral sites

7.1.1
.

For a transformation of geometries
\Phi
we want to describe explicitly the mate

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\int\Phi{\bf Spec}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle{\bf Spec}_{1}\int\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\Phi}}

where
\int\Phi
denotes the morphism of opfibrations between the bicategories of modelled topoi for the respective geometries and also its restriction to the bicategories of locally modelled topoi.

For
(\mathcal{F},F)
a
\mathbb{T}_{2}
-modelled topos, recall first that we have in each

in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
an equality
\Phi[\mathcal{F}]_{*}F(c)=\Phi[\mathcal{S}]_{*}(F(c))
. Then observe that for
(c,n)
in the site

, any choice of pushout square for
n
in
